COUNTY BOROUGH OF WARRINGTON. WAR REFUGEES FUND. The House Committee very gratefully aeknow- lesige the generous response of. the inhabitants of Warrington to their request for furniture, bedding, and other articles, which have enabled the Committee to completely furnish and equip St. Austin's ready for the reception of refugees. The House Committee would like to feel that they are in a position to maintain the home, and to provide the necessary food and comforts, and they would be glad to receive weekly contri- butions, however small, for that purpose. PETER PEACOCK, Mayor.

never before remarked. For instance, one model is so constructed that it can be worn on no fewer than four quite different occasions, and yet be perfectly suitable for each. Thus its cost is justified, moreover, the gown will not be thrown aside when scarcely half worn, but be practically serviceable to the end of the season. This model can be worn as a smart afternoon frock, from which a slight modification con- verts it into a tea gown. Some furthAr manipu- lation renders it suitable for dinner at a restaurant, and another slight change trans- forms it into an evening or theatre dress. It is capable of still further variety by a change of underskirt, should this be considered desir- able.

Sleeves and Hip Yokes. Sleeves have not so far undergone much change, and as they may be considered the fore- runners of modes, it is safe to predict that dras- tic changes in styles are not contemplated, at any rate for the autumn. Both the Raglan and Kimons have been amplified and modified, and the former is so comfortable a shape that it will remain in favour as long as the baggy corsage prevails. Some sleeves have the Raglan_ shoul- der, combined with the fulness of the Kimons, as far as the waistline. There is a marke,d ten- dency in all directions towards a suppression uf exaggeration. The effect of bulk on the hips is quite cleverly avoided, more especially in the case of pleated skirts—which in plaids and tar- tans are very popular—by means of yokes, deep or shallow, that take away unbecoming fulness, and the material of some of the underskirts is so arranged that only the sides appear fuller.

The Pelerine Returns. Probably the war is responsible for the popu- larity of the cheaper peltry this autumn, at any rate the London houses are showing far more of the inexpensive furs this year than last. The skins of the wolf, Kolinski sable, squirrel and natural musquash are so wonderfully treated that they can scarcely be recognised. Wolf is now so silky and fine in texture that it looks like the best fox. Kolinski resembleim sian sable, and skunk may well be mist—en for

Bottling Fruit (no sugar). Fill bottles with fruit and cold water (boiled), put the lids on, raise temperature slowly in about 11 hours) to 160 degrees F. Beep at this temperature for 11 hours, take oft and cool down slowly.

Bottling Plums (a little sugar). An old Surrey recipe is: Choose ripe plums, prick all over with a fork, put them into bottles with a little sugar, leave uncovered till the sugar has dissolved; then seal with melted mutton fat. Plums so treated taste as if brandied.

HERE AND THERE.

In Grave Danger. Refused compensation for a cut finger, a domestic servant left her situation without notice, and wrote the following letter to her late mistress : "Madam,—The cut is worst. The doctor says I have cut the spinal cord of my little finger. If you do not immediately send me five shillings a week I shall insult my solicitor."

The Whole Code. Emily,: Why are you waving your handker- chief? Angelina: Since papa has forbidden Tom the house we have arranged a code of signals.

Emily: What is it? Angelina: When he waves his handkerchief five times that means "Do you love me?" And when I wave frantically in reply it means "Yes, darling." Emily: And hor do you ask other questions? Angelina: We don't. That's the whole code. Poor Blinks. Blinks, after inviting his friend Jinks, who has just returned from abroad, to dinner, is telling him what a fine memory his little son Bobby has. "And do you suppose he will remember me?', said Jinks. "Remember you? Why, he remembers. every face that he ever saw." An hour later they enter the house and after Jinks has shaken hands with Mrs. Blinks, he calls Bobby over to him. "And do you remember me, my little man?" "Course I do. You're the same feller that pa brought home last summer and ma was so wild about it that she didn't speak to pa for a whole week."

THE BUGLER. Amongst the first batches of British wounded soldiers to arrive in England from the battle- fields on the Continent was a young bugler of about 17 years of age. This brave boy had lost his left arm, whilst both his legs and his head were covered with bandages. One of his friends, also a wounded soldier, said that the young hero "stuck it," and he had " four bullet wounds in him before he finished 'sounding his bugle." As he was lifted into the ambulance he still held his bugle in his remaining hand.

LORD ROBERTS'S LETTER To YOU. Lord Roberts has written a letter to all the children of the British Empire. The message, which is for publication in the "League of the Empire Magazine," reads You have all heard of the War; you have all heard of the fighting forces sent from every part of the Empire to help the Mother Country. Why are we fighting? Because the British Empire does not break its pro- mises, nor will it allow small nations to be

Now, the British Government promked, with all the Great Powers of Europe, IncluJ- Ing Germany, that no army should set foot on the territory of the little nation of Belgium without her leave; in other words, she "guaranteed the neutrality of Belgium." Germany, however, was bent on war, and on dominatimg other nations. Britain did her best to keep the peace, but Germany (break- ing her word) marched her ,armies into Bel- gium to try and conquer /France. Children of the Empire, this is why we are at war—to hold our promise, to help our friends, and to keep the Flag of Liberty flying, not only over our own Empire, but over the whole world. God save our King and Empire.

THE KAISER'S MISTAKE. (By JESSIE The proud Kaiser thought he could conquer the world. My Word, How very absurd! He said that Old England's flag soon would De, furled, But No! _ _

That could not be so. For the English are brave as they ever have been: They will die for their country, their King and their Queen. So the pride of the Kaiser must suffer a fall. Sad? 'Yes. But righteous' I guess. Ah, Perhaps he will never recover at all! • Well, One never can tell. And all men must bow 'neath the chastening rod, Who cannot conform with the laws of their God. •

CHAT and GOSSIP.

By MARJORIE.

A British Princess in Germany. The Grand Duchess of Mecklenburg-Strelitz, who is now in her ninety-second year, loses, for the time being, her British pension of three thousand a year through the war. She is the only living grandchild of George 111., and is particularly attached to Britain, the land of her birth. The war will be a great grief to her. The loss of her pension will be of little conse- quence, as the House to which her husband be- longed is, next to the Hohenzollerns, the richest in Germany. It is said that quite half the duchy belongs to the reigning Duke, who is her grandson.

Home-made Fabrics for Spring Garments. American dressmakers and buyers for the big stores had this year to get their autumn models from Paris in London. Many novelties had ar- rived before the war broke out, and others were taken by motor car to the coast, packed in. suit cases, by assistants who went continually back- wards an forwards. Spring fashions are now occupying the attention of designers, and the materials for gowns and other garments—it is satisfactory to know—Will be made chiefly in these islands, because most of the French fac- tories are closed, the employes, being at the front.

An Adaptable Gown. This season's fashions are fittingly charac- terised by a well-chosen simplicity, whicl, in many instances tends to a certain quaint pic- turesqueness that is particularly pleasing and attractive. Comfort and economy have also been studied, and designers have turned their attention to adaptable garments to a degree

HOME, NOTES. Profitable Recipes and Useful Suggestions.

Butter gives energy and power to work to those who eat heartily of it. Placed in a bottle of dried-up glue, vinegar will moisten and make it liquid again. Tar may be removed from the hands by rub- bing with the outside of fresh orange or lemon peel and drying immediately. The volatile oils dissolve tar so that it can be rubbed off. It is a good plan to lay a thick sheet of white blotting paper under the covers of dressing- tables and sideboards. This will absorb any liquid that may be spilt before it can reach and harm the wood.

Rice Buns. ilb. butter, Vb. sugar, Vb. flour, 20z. ground rice, 1 teaspoonful baking powder, 2 eggs, cry- stallised sugar. Cream the butter and sugar together by beating these two ingredients until the sugar is quite dissolved in the butter and the mixture resembles thick cream. Add the eggs well beaten, and stir in the flour, ground rice and baking powder. Form the mixture into small balls, press these flat into some crystal- lised sugar, and bake on a buttered baking sheet for ten minutes in a quick oven.

Stewed Mutton With Onions. Take half a leg of mutton (the knuckle end), ten large onions, one tablespoonful of flour, pep- per, and salt, one gill of hot water, and one gill of milk. Peel and scald the onions, cut them into thin slices, place half at the bottom of a saucepan, sprinkle over a little salt, place the mutton on the onions, and cover completely with the remainder of the onions. Add a little more salt and pepper, pour on one gill of hot water, cover with a very tight lid, and let all stew by the side of the fire for three or four hours, or until the onions are quite soft and the mutton .-well done. Lift out the mutton on to a hot dish, mash the onions, mix the flour with the milk free from lumps, add it to the onions, stir until boiling, and then smother the mutton with the sauce, and serve hot.

Tomato Soup. Put into a pan lb. of tomatoes with one pint of stock, water, or the water in which vege- tables have been cooked, one bayleaf, a spray of parsley, a stick of celery (the outside leaves of a head of celery answer admirably for this). six peppercorns, and teaspoonful of sugar, and simmer until the tomdtoes are soft. Meanwhile, fry a sliced onion in 1 oz. of well-clarified drip- ping, taking care not to let it colour; then add

MY DEAR CHILDREN,— How nice it would be if we could make sunshine! We would stop the clouds from making a dull day, and we would always live in brightness. But, of course, we cannot do this. There is, however, another way of making brightness. It can be done by helping others who are not so well off as ourselves, or by help- ing those who are in trouble. This is a grand thing to do, and any such help will have its reward later, although we! - should never think of any reward when we are doing a good turn. We should only think of the happiness we are making. This is what the scholars at our Secondary School thought when they decided to make a collection of things which could be sent to the soldiers who are fighting in France. They also! made little articles of clothing for. the homeless children from brave little Belgium. These children need comforting, for many of them have lost their fathers and in some cases their mothers, too. Are you doing your share to make sunshine for these homeless boys and girls? Are you doing your share to make the soldiers happy? You can do it just as well as the scholars at the Secondary School. Tell your teacher that YOU would like to give a little present, and no doubt he (or she) will be very pleased to col- lect your gifts. Mention this to your schoolmates. I am cer- tain that they, too, would like to help. Have you got any more members for the corner?

THE FIRST GUNS. The Germans were the inventors of the first can. About 1378 Schwartz, a German machin- ist, manufactured numerous crude guns, which were brought into use by the Venetians in 1392. It is a strange fact that cannons were made before small firearms. Cannons were first used in war at the battle of Crecy in 1346. It was not until 1544 that they were made in England.

The Smallest Watch in the World.

Women have taken to wearing fobs, ,either as pendants on corsages or on. long waistcoats, where they are more fittingly placed. A tiny watch, set vfith diamonds, is a novelty expressly made to suspend from a• fob. TICS jewellery trade of Paris has, in consequence of the war, been transferred to London, consequently a number of novelties have made their appearance in our metropolis much earlier than would have been the case under normal conditions. A very wonderful watch, probably the smallest in the world, has arrived here. Although less than half an inch in diameter, this exquisite piece of craftmanship is perfect, and keeps good time. A valuable diamond forms its setting, the watch being at the back of the gem.

Cheap Apples. , The apple crop this year has been an excep- tionally large one, and the palm for producing the biggest single specimen appears to belong to Devonshire, with a single apple' weighing 34}oz. This is slightly heavier than the record one last year, which hailed from the county of Hereford, and was sold in Covent Garden market for six- teen guineas. Since we hicie received almost constant supplies all the year round of fine colonial apples, the art of, storing and keeping home-grown produce for winter use has been ilk much neglected. In Canad^ -1. popular way is to pare, quarter and core tl,- .les, and thread the pieces on fine string, v,, a. is afterwards stretched across a drying room. Each piece is kept from contact with another, and when re- quired is washed and steeped in water over- night before cooking.

HINTS ON HEALTH. Useful Information in 'Popular

Language.

Beneficial Frugality. There are few families in the kingdom who, by giving a little thought to the matter, could not spend less money on food and drink and at the same time benefit materially in health and strength and vitality, writes Dr. Olsen in "Good Health." Luxurious eating and drinking and the gratification of a perverted appetite means equally a waste of money and loss of health. A frugal and inexpensive diet not only makes for clear thinking, but also for good health.

Advantages of Fruit. Fruit, fresh or stewed, is always a valuable food, even though it may not contain a large amount of nutrition. The pleasant salts and acids have a gentle medicinal effect upon the stomach, bowels and liver, which is most con- ducive to health. But it is not necessary to get the expensive fruits. The apple, the king of fruits, is a most wholesome and delicious fruit, and can be taken fresh, baked or stewed. Re- , member the old adage, "an apple a day keeps the doctor away." The banana is a more nutri- tious food, and possesses many virtues. There is also the lowly date, a veritable food for the gods, and the fig equally nourishing and sus- taining. But these are only a few of the many fruits, home-grown or foreign, which are readily obtained at comparatively small ex— pense.

The Best Drink. Dr. Olsen, after lamenting that the nation wastes £160,000,000 a year upon alcoholic drinks Which do no good, but an infinite amount of harm, says :—We must not fail to say a word about the increasing use of so-called temper- ance drinks, such as ginger ale or beer, soda water, various bottled lemonades and similar preparations. While these drinks may he ?..Om- paratively innocent and harmless, yet who will maintain that they are necessary or essential to good health and physical fitness? Certainly not. Therefore we invite our temperance friends and total abstainers to discard these unnecessary and useless drinks and to resort to Adam's ale—the genuine aqua vita or water of life. There is no more satisfying drink than water. Artificially prepared drinks have the effect of stimulating rather than quenching thirst. Most fortunately we possess everywhere in the United Kingdom a pure and wholesome water Supply, and this is the drink that we recommend to all, rich and poor, feeble and strong, sick and well.

Sir William de Whittington, of Pauntley, Gloucestershire. Sir Richard lived in London in 1379, when he gave money towards a city loan. He was thrice Lord Mayor of London, and until his death in 1432 he took a leading Pa' t in London affairs and was knighted by King Henry V. for his financial services. The part of the story relating to the cat probably took its origin from his owning a ship named "Cat.'

THE BANK MANAGER. It is a very anxious time for the manager of a bank when the public have lost confidence in the concern and rash to draw all their money out. A great number of managers have been in this unfortunate situation, but the clever trick of one of them deserves mention. This incident occurred many years ago in a little town in South Wales. The farmers who had money in this particular bank clamoured at the doors for their money back. Things, were going from bad to worse. However, the manager was a clever man, and by his cleverness he saved the situation. __ „ _

"John," he said to his chief clerk, "I want you to help me out of this trouble. You must get some of the sovereigns from the safe and put them in a frying pan over the fire." The chief clerk did as he was told and passed them to the manager. The manager threw the hot money on- the ounter towards a big farmer named Williams, who wanted all his money out of the bank. "There Mr. Williams," said the manager, "that's your money." The farmer in picking up the sovereigns burnt his fingers, and then he shouted,," They are quite hot; what do you. mean?" "Of course they are hot," said the manager;

"what else can you expect They are only just out of the Mould. We are making them in hun- dreds as fast as we can."

"Making them!'" asked the farmer. "If that is so, there is no need to draw my money out. There is no fear of the money running short." This gave confidence to all the customers and one by one they left the offices thinking it was better to leave their money in the bank.

BISHOP WELLDON'S HOPES.

" When the agony is passed," said Bishop Welldon at the Manchester Cathedral, "I hope that the old doctrine of the balance of power in Europe will be abandoned for ever. I hope there will be no more Triple Alliances or Triple Ententes and no more antagonism of Powers federated against one another." "If it should happen," he added "that Poland is restored, that Alsace and Lorraine are given back to France, and Bosnia and Herzegovina allowed to follow the bent of their national sympathies; if in Russia the Poles and the Jews be given their; liberty and deliv- ered from a persecution which defeats its own ends; then, perhaps, out of the turmoil there will emerge a providential benefaction."

FARM AND GARDEN.

HOW COTTAGERS CAN HELP. Great as are the resources of this country, generous as is the promise of a fruitful autumn, we know that it is possible to increase our home supplies greatly. In one way or another the subject of intensive cultivation has been urged for years past. We have made long strides, despite the difficulties, but there is much yet to be done. Now the time has arrived when we may be faced with the dire necessity of rais- ing the greater part of our own food. Even supposing our wheat imports to be assured, we cannot live by wheat alone, and there is a sullen forecast on the horizon that winter may, find us with all farm and garden products at prices which mean the pinch of hunger to millions. I need not say more. Let the small man, the cottager with his littlarden, remember that his efforts will be, as They have been many a time before, of no less national importance than those of larger cultivators. Just as the patient peasantry of France have ever been the founda- tion and buttress of their nation, just as they or their wives and children are to-day gallantly supporting the motherland against the aggres- sor by careful civation, so the cottagers and m sallholders of these islands can do their part by making the most of what land they may hap- pen to possess.—" Agricultural Economist and Horticultural Review."

===== 0002601_19141010_art0022.txt | included=true | included =====
TRAPPING MOLES.

Finding the right rune is three parts of the battle. The main runs are the only ones to set traps in, no matter whether wood or iron be used. They are nearly always found near hedges or boundaries of fields, and very often on river or brook banks, or even round a pond. A sharp spike to tap the ground will tell. Just on the ridge of a bank is a pretty sure place for a good "set," which should nearly clear a field. Such thoroughfares as these are used all the year round, and may be " set " at any time. Iron traps are the best to use. Where the mole proves too cunning for even careful setting, the plan is to jug him. It is managed in this way: Dig a hole below bottom of run big enough to hold a jug—one with a good shoulder—place jug in space, and cover up the run quite securely w, for into the jug he goes, from the light. Nothing can save him when he passes next that ay, and can in no wise get out again with his short legs and open feet, as the inside of the jug is so smooth.

COMMANDEERING OF FARM HORSES. It was a new thing among us when Govern- ment officials swooped down and asked us for the use of many of our best horses for war•pur- poses. Here and there a little grumbling could be heard, yet on the whole farmers faced the situation very cheerfully, and now after a few weeks they have as a rule found that the animals taken could be very well spared. I v , for the remount officials were, said the horses were asked for, and used the word adisedly, as a rule, very considerate and did not display their arbitrary powers until absolutely neces- sary. There were not many cases of hardship, and these were such as the taking of one of two or three where it could be ill spared, and the price allowed has been a sore question in some cases. This, however, is a matter for future settle- ment, as farmers who have parted with horses not previously registered and valued can Claim any just difference in value over the price now paid.—" Agricultural Economist and Horticul- tural Review."

===== 0002601_19141010_art0023.txt | included=true | included =====
IN THE GARDEN.

VARIETIES OF TURNIPS TO GROW.

The quality Of the yellow-fleshed turnips is, to my mind, says a writer in "Farm and Home," superior to that of the wlbite varieties, while it will be found that they also keep much better. Those who have not grown the yellow- fleshed varieties should certainly give them a trial. August is generally recommended for sowing these, but in the south good results have followed a September sowing. Too early sowing is not advisable, as a shapely root of the size of a cricket ball is far better than one much larger. Such roots also keep longer, and are far better as regards flavour. In Scotland the variety most generally grown is Golden Ball, one of the most shapely, yellow-fleshed varieties, very solid, keeping well, and of a deep yellow colour. Should any reader have a shallow soil to deal with, then Yellow Perfection, which is flatter, will be found to answer well. This has a very short top, and is of excellent quality, while it comes into use much earlier than the round varieties. The old and well-known Orange Jdlly also deserves a place. There is no lack of varieties, but those of compact growth and that keep well are to be preferred.

A PURE FOOD. For the fifth successive year " ATORA" Re- fined Beef Suet has been awarded the Certificate of the Incorporated Institute of Hygiene for the highest Standard of Quality. Although "ATORA" contains no preservative, it keeps sweet for months. No chopping required; goes nearly twice as far as raw Suet. Your grocer sells it—Shredded (coated with rice flour) for puddings and pastry and in blocks for frying and cooking. Ask for "ATORA," refuse substituted brands.

IBER 10, 1914.

MARKET-GATE CIRCUS.

The Value of a Shop in Dispute. ARBITRATION PROCEEDINGS.

Arising out of the Market Gate Circus Scheme arbitration proceedings of considerable public interest took place at the Town Hall on Wed- nesday. Among the premises that will be affected by the street-wLdening process necessary in order to lend a touch of dignity to the appearance of what will, no doubt, be regarded in future as the shopping-hub of Warrington( is a shop and house, No. 7, Buttermarket-street, in which the Rev. Joseph Howard has a life interest. The purpose of the arbitration was to deter- mine what compensation should be paid to the reverend gentleman for the compulsory acqui- sition of part of the property in which he is interested. The arbitration proceedings were of a protracted character. Mr. Edward Holmes, of Sheffield, was the umpire, and Mr. J. W. Hanson, of Hudders- field, acted as arbitrator for the Corporation, Mr. John Mainwaring, of Warrington, officiat- ing in a like capacity for the claimant. The case for the Corporation was argued by Mr. Gordon Hewart, K.C., M.P. (acting on the instructions of the Town Clerk), whilst Mr. A. R. Kennedy appeared for the claimant.

===== 0002601_19141010_art0025.txt | included=true | included =====
WARRINGTON A GREAT BUSINESS _CENTRE.

In opening the proceedings Mr. Kennedy said that the Rev. Joseph Howard, who lived at Sankey Hall, was the tenant for life of the property, the remainder, at his death 'passing to ,his son, who unfortunately was a permanent invalid. The premises consisted of a shop, a basement, a room above the shop, two rooms on the top storey, 'a two-floor building at the back, and a yard, and the usual offices. At tlf time when the notice to treat was served the shop was an open-fronted one, because it had been used as a butcher's 'shop., The shop front which was suggested in the (plans before the arbitrators had been put in by the Corporation for the purpose of temporarily accommodating the lady who at present carried on business on the premises, whose former shop had been already taken. The frontage, measured inside the walls, was 14 feet. The value of the position of the shop could be pretty well gauged from observation, but, in point of measurement, it was just 27 yards' from the junction of the four main streets—the four main arteries of Warrington and of the district in the centre of which War- rington stands. In fact, the four road; in question were of very great importance to the county—the road on the west leading to Liver- pool, that on the east to Manchester, that co 3 the north to Wigan and Preston, and that on the south to Chester and Crewe. Warrington was unquestionably the centre of great indus- trial activity and also of agricultural interests, and it could not be denied by anybody that the record of the town was one of continuous growth and development. The number of inhabited houses, the population and the rateable value had increased continuously and were 'still in- creasing. The natural importance of the site on which these premises was situated, was in- creased by the fact that, within recent years, the tramway service had been developed and was instrumental in bringing shoppers from the outside to the centre of the town, thereby having an appreciative effect upon the value of central property. The shop was situated in the neighbourhood that might fairly be de- scribed as the shopping quarter and the centre of very considerable human activity, particu- larly at certain times of the week. One had only to look round to see the character of the shops in the neighbourhood, even though demo- lition had taken place to some extent, with accompanying injury to existing property.

===== 0002601_19141010_art0026.txt | included=true | included =====
The Terms of Tenancy._

As an indication of the value of the premises, it might be mentioned that they came into the possession of the family of Mr. Howard in 1878, and that at that time they were let at £5O a year for a period of seven years. In 1884 an arrangement was come to by which a new lease of ten years was granted to Messrs. G. and T. Bowcock, who were well-known butchers at that time. When that lease of ten years ran out in 1894, it was renewed for a further ten at the same rent and on the same terms, viz., that the I tenant was responsible for internal repairs. Mr. Howard thought of asking for an increase of rent, but for certain reasons did not do so, one reason being that he was completely satisfied with the tenant, and another, the fact that the tenant had nine children. A third reason was that he was the mortgagee of the premises. In 1904, when the second period of ten years ex- pired, Mr. Howard asked the tenant for a rental of £7O a year. The tenant was prepared to pay that sum, but, after certain negotiations, the premises were re-let to Mr. Bowcock at a rental of £6O a year. In March, 1912, Mr. Howard gave the tenant notice to quit, one of his reasons for doing so being that he intended to spend a certain amount of money on improving the shop, by putting in a new front, &c., With a view to getting a bigger rental than the then tenant was prepared to pay. That notice would have expired in March of the present year, but in August of last year came the notice to treat, which brought to an end any idea of improving the property, and, as a secondary consequence, when Mr:- Bowcock quitted the shop in March. 1913, the premises remained empty for a period of nearly eleven months, viz., until February, 1914, when the Corporation took them tempo- rarily, agreeing to pay therefore 5 per cent. on the amount of the award. For thirty-four years the premises had never been unoccupied for a single day, and for twenty-eight years out of that period they were held by one person, or firm, at a rent which certainly increased, al- though it probably was not the rack rent. The consideration arising from those facts was that the property on the date of the notice to treat had become a very valuable asset, bearing a good reputation, which in a measure arose from the fact that it had been successfully held by a well-known firm of family butchers for 28 ' years, which conferred upon it a local good- will, so to speak. The notice to treat was given as long ago as August, 1912, and here they were, more than two years later, considering what should be paid to the owner. The delay which had taken place in the completion of the Coun- cil's scheme had had a deleterious effect upon , properties generally in the neighbourhood.

Rents Steadily Going Up. Nobody could contest the proposition that rents had been steadily increasing in this neigh- bourhood, or that it was one in which rents were likely to continue, to rise and steadily ap- preciate. The more means of communication developed—and they had been doing that in recent years—the more valuable would property in the centre of the town become, no matter what might happen on the outskirts. The pro- perty in its present condition obviously re- quired some repairs, but an expenditure of £lOO on a new shop front and .in general repairs would make the plate quite an attractive one. The estimated value was not based upon any improvement that would result from the expen- diture of such a sum, but was founded upon the condition of the property at the time of the treat. The evidence would show that the rental of the property—which, as they knew, was a few years ago let for £6o—might fairly be stated as .t7O, less 10 per cent. for insurance, repairs, &c., which would reduce the total to £63 net. Com- pensation at 4 per cent. on 25 years' purchase would yield £1,575, to which 10 per cent. must be added for compulsory purchase, making a total of 41,732. On top of that the sum of ..t52 10s, for rent lost during the ten months from the 20th of March,. 1913, to February 2nd, 1914, must be added. The property was an im- proving one commercially; it was a good pro- perty with a good record and with excellent prospects of further improvement, and in esti- mating its value he suggested that the fair thing to do would be to take into account not only what the eye saw, but also what the mind could foretell, viz. the prospects of subsequent im- provement 4:nd the potentialities of the place. In putting the rental value at £7O per annum, it could not be said that the claimant erred on the side of generosity to himself, and, as those who had seen the property would agree, it was quite easy to conceive that with comparatively small expenditure an 'increased rent could be obtained.

THE TOWN STEADILY IMPROVING. Mr. J. D. Wallis, a member of the firm of Messrs. Dunlop, Wallis and Co., valuers, &e., of Manchester, said he had been acquainted with the Warrington district for the last 23 years. The town was not only an important manufacturing oentre, but was also an impor- tant market town, serving a large outside agri-

cultural district. All indications poirgd to improvement in the town—the population was steadily and consistently growing, as was also the rateable value. The construction of the Corporation tramways tended to bring people into the centre of the town for business pur- poses, the result being that shop values there had readily and steadily increased, and that it had been the custom from time to time to raise rents. Dealing with the history of the property, witness said that it was most impor- tant to remember that for 34 years the owner had not lost a single week's rent, and he put the compensation payable' at £1,785. Answer-. ing Counsel for -the Corporation, the witness said that the premises were old, but the main walls were quite good. He did not agree that .18 years' purchase would be right, but if the property were situate two miles out of the town he would concede that that was a fair offer:

Mr. Henry Hartley, surveyor, Sze., of Liver, pool, stated that he had been acquainted with Warrington and district for the past 35 or 40 years, and had had to deal with a number of properties in the town which were compul- sorily acquired by the C9rporation in connec- tion with-the widening scheme. Trade was, temporarily, seriously depressed, whilst the widening operations were being carried through.

===== 0002601_19141010_art0027.txt | included=true | included =====
CLAIM FOR £2,340.

The Rev. J. Howard was next called. He was, he said, the tenant for life of the property in question, which was his wife's, the remain- der being to his son. The property. came into the possession of his wife's family in 1878, and she became the owner in 1884. He would like to bear witness to Mr. Bowcock's excellence as a tenant—he was a very fine .tenant and no landlord could wish for a better. Witness always had a soft heart, and he. dealt with Mr. Bowcock very tenderly; he was led to do so by certain reasons, and also by the fact that the gentleman mentioned was doing his very best to make his business a success at a time when trade was not particularlfgood. He said to Mr. Bowcock : "I want to live and you want to live! Would a rent of £6O serve you?" and Mr. Bowcock replied "Yes." He continued to pay that until witness .gave him notice—not from any dissatisfaction with him as a tenant. Mr. Kennedy: The fact that he had stayed with you for 28 years speaks for itself! In. answer to Mr. Hewart, the witness said that when, in March, 1912, he gave Mr. Bow- cock notice to quit, he came round and asked that what had occurred might be. overlooked and petitioned to be allowed to remain on at £6O a year. Mr. Hewart : What I suggest is that before you gave him notice to quit you again sug- gested that the rent should be raised and that he would not agree to pay more than £6O a year, and that, therefore, you ordered him to leave?

Witness: You are altogether wide of the mark Witness added that he did not think that the question of an increase was ever again raised between him and his, tenant prior to the giving of notice. Ile (witness) was so well satisfied with his teniint, who, at the time, was rising in business and securing a good con- nection. Answering further questions, the reverend gentleman said that the claim he sent to the Corporation was for .t 2,340 which was the sum named by his valuer.

===== 0002601_19141010_art0028.txt | included=true | included =====
CORPORATION VALUER SAYS £955.

Called on behalf of the Corporation, - Mr. James Henry Stephens, of Leigh, who said he had an intimate acquaintance with selling and valuing in Warrington, gave it as his opinion that the building in question was in such a condition that in a few years the owner would have to strip the site. The front wall al)- peered to be especially had and was consider- ably bulged. The property was old and ready for reconstruction.

Mr. Hewart : In your opinion, is there any foundation for the suggestion that by the ex- penditure of some capital upon the existing building an increased rent could be obtained? Witness: I do not think that is a practical, feasible proposition. Continuing, witness said that the maximum rent that could be ob- tained from the premises, in his opinion, was .t6O. Rather than there being a reasonable prospect of any appreciation of rent, he thought there would be a diminution because old pro- perty of that kind would become less and less desirable. A tenant would only look at that property after he had exhausted the list of all the other properties to let in the locality. The property was not now in much a condition as to command a rent of .160, and-in arriving at the amount of compensation to be paid the rent ought to be capitalised at not more than 18 years, and £955 would be a fair and proper sum to award. He fixed the period at 18 years because the property was old, and there was a prospect of its ceasing to become usable. Answering Mr. Kennedy, the witness said that he thought the premises would be very well let in their present state if they fetched £55 a year.

===== 0002601_19141010_art0029.txt | included=true | included =====
ANOTHER SAYS £925.

Mr. Ernest Gunson, of Manchester, the valuer to the Assessment Committee, said he had visited the premises. No increase could be made in the size of the building now existing on account of limitations of light. The building was in a very bad condition indeed, the roof was faulty and the front wall bulged out into the street. A rent of £6O a year was the utmost that could, in witness's opinion, be obtained for the premises, and Aitness could see no reason for expecting an increase on that figure. He considered that £925 would be a fair and reasonable valuation.

THE ISSUE BEFORE THE ARBITRATORS. This was all the evidence and counsel then addressed the umpire and arbitrators. Mr. Hewart, speaking first, said some attempt had been made, perhaps by accident, to intro- duce into the case certain topics of prejudice. He was quite sure that would be disregarded by those engaged in the arbitration; they had noth- ing to do with the softness of heart of the rev. gentleman who Owned the property, nor with the fact that his son was a permanent invalid. The question was a straight one, and resolved itself into this : What is the fair sum the rate- payers of Warrington ought to be called upon to pay for the premises? Generosity was all very well in its way, but generosity at the expense of the Corporation would be entirely misplaced. The questions really resolved themselves into three—What wals the true rent .of the premises; what was the proper deduction to be made for compensation purposes from the rent paid; and what Was the proper number of years' purchase that ought to be given? His (counsel's) submis- sion was that when the rev, gentleman got £6O a year for the shop he was getting the full rack rent, and that it was purely conjectural to say that in the future there might be some increase. In his address to the arbitrators Mr. Kennedy asked them in assessing the compensation to be paid to take into account the fact that there was a peculiar demand for shop property in the part of the town in which the preperty in ques- tion was situated. The premises had never been in the market, and therefore their market price had never been fixed by competition. It was quite conceivable that the rent which was actually being paid at. the time Mr. Bowcock left was not the rack _rent. The house itself was, of course, in want of repair, but it was not incapable of improvement. It Would con- tinue for many years yet to be a valuable struc- ture, but, of course, the most important part of it was the shop. The decision will be announced later.

===== 0002601_19141010_art0030.txt | included=true | included =====
LESS UNEMPLOYMENT.

There has been a further decline in unem- ployment figures. For insured trades the rate on October 2 was 5.1, as compared with 5.4 a week ago and 6.3 a month ago. As regards general unemployment, the numbers on the books of the Labour Exchanges were 64,915, as compared with 66,954 a week ago.

WALTON PARISH CHURCH.

Harvest thanksgiving services were conducted in Walton Church on Sunday by the Vicar, the Rev. L. J. T. Darwall in the morning, and the Rev. Edward Gunter in the evening.

===== 0002601_19141010_art0036.txt | included=true | included =====
HARVEST COMPENSATIONS.

The Vicar took for his text "Give us this day our daily bread," and said that, were our Heavenly Fattier to cease giving, we would have nothing. Therefore we left everything to Him with the petition contained in the words of the text, and, though ten thousand hearts might cry for "daily bread," the Giver might deaf differently with each case. But, in His infinite goodness, He would care for each. The harvest forced the thought of our utter de- pendelice; we might easily forget that life wag the gift of God were it not that it had to be sustained by food. The harvest of the whole world had never yet failed, and they trusted that by Divine Providence it never would. When one countryes harvest was below the average the harvest in another exceeded the average, and the world was satisfied. The choir gave a fine rendering of the anthem "Joy in harvest" (Bruce Sterne), Master A. Greenough takinkthe. solo part. Mr. Frank Booth presided at the organ, and Mr. W. Gornall also accompanied on the cornet. Mr. H. J. Westbrook read the lessons.

In the evening the anthem Lovely appear" (Gounod) Was sung, Master A. Greenough again singing the solo part.

===== 0002601_19141010_art0037.txt | included=true | included =====
CAIRO-STREET UNITARIAN

CHAPEL.

The Cairo-street Unitarian Chapel harvest thanksgiving services were conducted on Sun- day by the Rev. Irvine Lister, the recently- appointed minister. The choir rendered special music at both services, the morning anthem being "Look on the fields, for they are white and ready to harvest" (Macpherson), and that in the evening " 0 lovely peace" (Handel). The solo parts were taken by 'Miss Mellor and Mrs. F. Monks, of Sankey, and Mr. J. M. Muir presided at the organ. The building had been beautifully, decorated with flowers and fruit by the ladies of the church, and there were large congregations. The collections, which were devoted to the Brotherly Fund, proved very satisfactory.

===== 0002601_19141010_art0038.txt | included=true | included =====
ELM WOOD-AVENUE CONGREGA- TIONAL CHURCH.

Services of thanksgiving for the harvest were conducted in the Elmwood-avenue Congrega- tional Church on Sunday by the Rev. C. P. Pitt. A number of ladies of the congregation had beautifully decorated the building with flowers and fruit, under the supervision of Mr. A. Ashton, and the attendances at all the services were excellent. In the morning the preacher took for his text Hosea xiv. 5: "And I will be as the dew unto Israel: he shall grow as the lily, and cast forth his roots as Lebanon." The prophet, lie said, was speaking to Israel of the days that were past. If they desired to be fruitful in the days yet to come, they must place themselves under His care. PRESENT-DAY CLOUDS.

The evening text was from Revelation xiv. 14 and 15: "And I looked, and behold a white cloud, and upon the cloud one sat like unto the Son of man, having on his head a golden crown, and in his hand a sharp sickle. And another angel came out of the temple, crying with a loud voice to him that sat on the cloud, Thrust in thy sickle, and reap: for the time is come for thee to reap; for the harvest of the earth is rige." The harvest, said the preacher, might be regarded in two ways. First there was the objective sense, in which they were the harvesters amongst men. Secondly, God was the great Harvester working amongst men, and they should use every opportunity for His pur- pose. The call to work in the harvest wotild come after preparation. The real meaning of the calling was that God's spirit should have free play in their own lives. Their minds were clouded at present by the terrible things hap- pening on the Continent, but to-morrow new work must be done, and God would give a call to them to send forth their sickle, and reap. The choir ably rendered the anthem "The spacious firmament" (Nichol), Mr. Reginald Wallington presiding at the 'organ. The day's collections amounted to £5 is. 6d.

The celebrations were concluded on Monday night with a social gathering, over which the Rev. C. P. Pitt presided. An enjoyable pro- gramme of songs and recitations was provided by Misses I. Pitt and Ethel 'wells, Mrs. A. E. Longshaw and Mr. W. H. Vrallington, whilst the choir gave two part-songs. During the even- ing the ladies of the church served refresh- ments, and the meeting concluded with a com- prehensive vote of thanks.

===== 0002601_19141010_art0039.txt | included=true | included =====
ST. JOHN'S PRESBYTERIAN.

The thanksgiving services at St. John's Pres- byterian Church on Sunday were attended by a large number of people. The Rev. W. Reid preached in the morning and evening, and at the latter service he was assisted by the Rev. J. J. Glover, of Risley. Special hymns and psalms were rendered by the choir, and in the morning Miss Ethel Hardcastle sang the solo "Hold Thou my hand," whilst in the evening Miss ounfield gave an impressive rendition of The Lord is my shepherd." The church was tastefully decorated, and the Pastor thanked all those who had assisted in bringing about such a desirable effect.

===== 0002601_19141010_art0040.txt | included=true | included =====
What the Churches Could Do.

-- In the evening the Rev. W. Reid took for his text the well-known words, "The kingdom of God is like a grain of mustard seed, which, when it is sewn in the earth, is less than all the seeds that be in the earth. But when it is sown, it groweth up and becometh greater than ! all the herbs and shooteth out great branches, so that the fowls of the air may lodge under the shadow of it," Mark iv. 31 and 32. Like the mustard seed, the kingdom of heaven, said the preacher, had small beginnings, but it was destined to grow until it assumed huge propor- tions. The idea of growth had always had a fascination for man; the idea of development from smaller to greater held humanity en- thralled. There was a continual growth whether in the life of men, nations or races, and men were never tired of studying it and seeing for them- selves. In the beginning the kingdom of heaven was the most insignificant thing to which one could point, but in the beginning whilst it was only small it was extremely vital. Insignificant it might be, but it was living and was an indis- pensable thing. In looking for and seeking bulk they all made the same mistake. They went in for big shops, cities, houses, salaries and lots of other big things. They wanted to be con- nected with big churches, to be ministers in big churches where there was a large communion roll; to have such a society inside the church that they could afford to have a select visiting list and something by .means of which they could enlarge their incomes; something that was big enough to take in all that. Whether it was vital or not, was a secondary consideration. Christ revealed the living God to the world, hence the vitality in Christianity. God was in I Christ, and therefore in the heart of Christian- ! ity-. They must not be concerned about the size or bulk of things, but about the living vital church, which grew from Within or not at all. The Christian Church was sufficient even in Warrington to raise the tone of public opinion, and it was doing that all over the world. If the church people wanted to close the public-houses they could do so to-morrow. They ' had the vitality if they would only put it forth. They could do whatever they set their hearts upon because of that vitality within them.

WELSH NONCONFORNIISTS AND THE WELSH ARMY CORPS.

The National Union of the Evangelical Churches of Wales have passed a resolution repudiating the allegations that Nonconform- ists were lukewarm towards recruiting, and decided to issue a manifesto in support of the Welsh Army Corps.

===== 0002601_19141010_art0043.txt | included=true | included =====
TILE

EXAMINER,'` SATURDAY,

CHURCHES AND THE WAR.

READINESS ESSENTIAL. Rev. Irvine Lister and the Struggle Before Us.

"To meet this present awful situation," writes the Rev. Irvine Lister, in the Cairo-street Chapel October Calendar, "great demands will be made upon our manhood. A long and arduous struggle is certain. Before the end is in sight none can tell what we may be called upon to bear, and it is essential that we should be ready. Although the sacrifices demanded of us have been great, they may yet be much greater. We cannot expect this terrible war to be concluded without much more loss of life than has occurred already. There may be re- verses and defeats greater than we have yet .known, and although we are confident of victory in the end, the time of waiting may be long and tedious and the strain almost unbearable. In the meantime, whilst the flower of our man- hood—on our behalf—is fighting for everything we hold dear, surely we ought to strengthen our spiritual life in order that we may be saved from excesses of violence, false judgments, un- charitableness and folly. If in time of peace we have need of communion with God, surely in this time of real danger we have need of God's constant presence, for the more deeply we plunge into strife the greater should be our dependence upon the Divine."

===== 0002601_19141010_art0044.txt | included=true | included =====
VICAR'S APPEAL FOR SERVICE. "Let Us All Be Ready To Do Our Share."

Writing on the war in the current number of the St. Ann's Parish Magazine, the Vicar (the' Rev. James Davies) says: "No one can foresee the end. The saddening and spirit-depressing effects of the war are felt on every side. It has already caused the bitterest and most unheard- of suffering, sorrow, anguish and bereavement to millions of many nations. Who does not feel the heart-pain caused by the unsheathing of the sword? We read and hear of peaceful citizens— without distinctions of age or sex—terrorised by armed marauders, maimed and slaughtered; of savage massacres by unchivalrous, unfeeling, barbarous men. . . . Thank God that our country has been spared these terrible scenes! Let us pray, and that often, and with the strongest faith and brightest hope, that our Lord God may stay the plague, shorten the con- flict, cause to cease for ever the causes of the war, and bring about an abiding peace. . . . Let us all be ready in every way to do our share in this great contest, by personal enlistment, by gifts to funds, by making articles for the comfort and protection of our sailors and sol- diers in their marches, in their trenches, and when/ they are sleeping in the open. Woollen socks, belts and sleeping caps are much in re- quest. . . . Let our share be indeed gifts of loyalty, lave and self-sacrifice. . . . Our cause is the cause of righteousness and our Lord God will uphold the right."

===== 0002601_19141010_art0045.txt | included=true | included =====
WYCLIFFE MEN AT THE FRONT. Twenty-five Fighting for King and Country.

An intercession service in time of war was conducted at Wycliffe Congregational Church on Sunday evening by the Rev. T. T. James. Before the sermon the preacher read out the following names of Wycliffe men serving in the Army at home and abroad: Richard Atkinson (Canadian forces), Tom Bennion, J. Bond, Tom Fogg, P. Ingham, A. Landor, Henry Law, H. Leather, W. H. Lowe, L. Muir, John Oxley, Will Pacey, A. Payne, H. Pickersgill, Gordon E. Pickton, Leonard Poole, S. Radcliffe, J. Sher- ratt, A. Spencer, C. Spencer, F. Spencer, W. Unsworth, Frank Wallington, Bert •Womersley and George T. Yeates. The Rev. T. T. James preached on " Th% Angel of the Night," taking for his text Acts xxvii. 22 and 23: " And now I exhort you to be of good cheer • for there stood by me this night the Angel of God, Whose I am, and Whom I serve." It was .good, he said, that they should come together to lay before God the whole affair of that terrible strife which was devastating Europe from end to end; and it was well that, when they prayed in that fashion, they should be quite certain that prayer was a real thing, and that there was a real God to Whom they prayed. It was because they were quite certain of that, that he now turned to the Bible for relief from the troubles of the times, to the story of the Apostle Paul. Paul was a man strong in what he was willing to surrender; he was a man who got mixed up in great things. He it was who started prayer meetings in a prison cell—lie was no milksop! No man had a greater record of pain and suffer- ing than the Apostle Paul, yet still he kept going on. What was it he never surrendered, so that through all pain and tribulation he still kept on, saying "Be of good cheer"? In the time of hardship and anxiety they had only to claim its aid, and there was a Presence that could help them in all their needs. That was a simple, elemental truth which was taught all through the Bible, and what was possible to Paul was possible also to them. So he (the preacher) wanted to say to all who were anxious, that the Angel of God could stand by them at home, and He could stand by their country if only they would seek Him. They must believe this, and perhaps a time of danger helped them to believe it, for one of the reasons why they found it so difficult to believe in God was because He made it so safe. And so it might be that these days of danger and anxiety and suffering would make them more sure of the Divine Presence, and, as they became more sure of it. restful and calm, brave and courageous, hopeful and certain of the days that were to come.

During the service Mrs. Smallshaw impres- sively rendered "Nearer, my God, to Thee," Mr. W. H. Payton presiding at the organ.

LONDON TO-DAY.

Questions That Interest the City

In War Tim.

BY A SPECIAL CORRESPONDENT.

German "News."

Much has been heard of late of the German system of "tuning the Press," and that in countries beyond Germany. Only a day or two ago I was admitted to know of this work in Denmark. There the comparatively small news agencies have, generally, succumbed to the pressure and patronage of the notorious Wolff Agency purporting to be independent. Now the Wolff Agency is only another face for the Berlin Foreign Office, and consequently it is only with much difficulty that war news, other than that edited in Berlin, is allowed to appear in Denmark. To get news into Den- mark not thus edited some ruses have to be reklrted to, and the Wolff Agency is ever on the pounce, ready to threaten those who pub- lish news which is not theirs. Since the war broke out we have seen more plainly how this agency works. It is semi- official; the Foreign Secretary at Berlin was present 'at its foundation, and in addition to the minimum of £5O to be contributed by each organisation joining the bureau, the Foreign Office at Berlin subscribes £12,500 a year. The result we see all around us in the world, but none more amusingly than when Prince von Bulow instructs the Swedes by means of the speech which the Right Hon. John Burns, M.P., did not deliver at the Albert Hall.

The Searchlights. The Londoner is, par excellence, a sightseer. He takes a genuine interest in the great war on the Continent, without question; but let there be no mistake about it, the Londoner is a sight- seer, even when . the reverberations of cannon can be heard from across the sea. lam re- minded of this because of the great heliographs, those searchlights which have been at work at night in London for the past few weeks, search- ing the sky for the possible Zeppelin, or other terror which prowleth at night. There is nothing very exhilarating about these great beams of light moving in a large circle; but your Londoner finds a novelty here, and couples and singles stand for hours at a time to witness the flash, the moving column from the top of Big Ben at the Parliament House, from the South-Eastern Railway Station, and from the top of the arch at Hyde Park Corner. These searchlights are among the things by which the multitude in London will remember the Great War of 1914.

Germany to Blame. Let me put on record for readers this passage from the report to Sir Edward Grey from Sir Maurice de Buraseu, recently British Ambas- sador at Vienna. The subject is the negotia- tions at Vienna immediately before the war broke out : Unfortunately these conversations at St. Petersburg and Vienna were cut short by the transfer of that dispute to the more danger- ous ground of a direct conflict bctween Ger- many and Russia. Germany intervened on the 31st July by means of her double ulti- matums to St. Petersburg and Paris. The ultimatums were of a kind to which only one answer is possible, and Germany declared war on Russia on the Ist August, and on Prance on the 3rd August. A few days delay might in all probability have saved Europe from one of the greatest calamities in history." Is not that a pregnant paragraph? How much does it reveal! The significance is great when it is compared with the first White Paper of correspondence, and that by Sir Edward Goschen recently at Berlin. Such a passage as that shows Germany determined to bring on a war; and should be kept by us as a witness to the cause and origin of this war.

Shop Windows as Schools. I pass a certain stationer's shop every morn- ing almost, and a very interesting exhibit is that window of his, full of all the curious and useful things which are seen in the stationery industry nowadays. Of course, most attractive of all are things touching the war. As I passed this morning, I observed a common sight now, viz., a young servant maid or nursery governess explaining war 'cards to two tots of six and eight, years or so. Of their curiosity and inter- est, why they were consuming; and we can depend upon it those lessons .were lessons for life and aye. But I am touching an old sub- ject, no doubt. The shop windows of London are an educating sight to anybody of any age and country. I should not give much for the man or woman who can go along our streetS and. look at these windows without getting a helpful suggestion. The shop windows, in short, are among our schools. What's In a Name?

A good deal of notice has been taken of the Russian change of the name of their famous newer capital from Petersburg to Petrograd. It ,has been explained that the ending grad is the Slavonic equivalent of the German burg; and the change' witnesses to the fervour of war time for Slavonic in all. But there is much more than meet i the eye in this matter, appar- ently so trivial, or the result of exalted patriotic feeling. •To Say that grad is an equivalent of burg may be correct enough, but what justifies the change and how much is the significance? It signifies the arrival of the Slav at manhood and independence in Europe. Russia, from Western European standpoints, has been re- garded for many years as the home of Asiatic hordes of semi-civilised peoples. They took their European designations largely from .1-er- many, and Peter the Great's city became Petersburg. It is always worth noting, when a people become self-assertive, and especially unashamed of their tongue, and of their insti- tutions. When so numerous a people as the

===== 0002601_19141010_art0047.txt | included=true | included =====
Keeping House & Keeping We

The woman who "keeps house" has enough to do when she is in good, sound health; but if she is weak, tired all the time, and suffering from morn to night with an aching back, house work becomes a heavy burden. Doan's Backache Kidney Pills have brought new life, health and strength to thousands of women afflicted in this way...

Women are subject to -kidney disease. The clothing they wear—the work they do, with its worry and strain—the lack of proper exercise— all teld to it. Backache, bearing-down pains, headache, dizziness, faintness, fits of " blues," are common symptoms of kidney disease. When any one of these ills appears, together with irregularities in the action of the kidneys, it is evidence that the kidneys are weak and require help. Doan's Backache Kidney Pills have helped thousands of weak women through trying times when kidney disease means so much added misery. They do not disturb stomach or bowels and contain no poisonous, dangerous nor habit- forming drugs. They are a safe medicine for women and men, and their success is due to the fact that the formula is good, and that Doan'i Pills are a special medicine for kidney an bladder diseases only.

DOAN'S

In 219 boxes only, six boxes 1319. A

Russians assert themselves, the world needs must attend. A word may be said of this in the Briton's ear; an ear which has listened too long to ideas begotten of the Crimean War largely. Here is that word—Let us think of Russians as men and brethren, and away with the idea that they are mere savages! Petrograd in future.

Premier's Unique Position.. A story is going the round of the clubs, it is said, that when war broke out the First Lord of the Admiralty told the Prime Minister, while still Minister of War', "that now war had broken out the Army and Navy should he under one head," to which the Prime Minister replied, "No, no, I cannot take on any more." Mr. Asquith's hold upon the House of Com- mons and of the country is unique. All parties acknowledge his domination, not a domination springing out of any autocratic rule, but arising out of the Strength and wisdom of his char- acter. In this war the country, one and indi- Visable,,\ trusts Asquith!

"Opened by Censor." Communication with the Continent, and to any country, is an adventurous sort of business. I have been reminded of that only a few minutes ago by a Danish friend of mine. The Danes to-day are friends of all, enemy of none They want to sell butter, bacon, and anything for an honest penny. But everybody is sus- pect; he may be only an indirect correspondent of a German merchant or other man. Hence the Censor at the Post Office comes into the play. My friend has just handed me a batch of newspapers and envelopes newly arrived from Copenhagen. All bear marks of examina- tion. The letters have been opened, every one of them, and a label pasted across the edge where they were opened, bearing the legend— " Opened by Censor "—in large letters. As I heard a working man say of another thing— "We must put up with it."

Plum Puddings Safe. War or no war, our plum puddings are safe! That is one of the momentous announcements of the day. The Christmas fruit season has opened, and the crop of Sultanas, Valencias, and Currants is said to be good and plentiful. So there is a comfort! But lest any think me ribald and avid for pudding, in these serious days, let me add that I am, and not because of my fondness for plum pudding, but I see in this announcement a suggestion of the fact for which we Britons should be so thankful. It is true that we are at war, in a very serious tussle, which may last long, though I do not think it will, but we are an island folk, possessed of a mighty Navy, and we are safe, and the sea whence we draw our supplies is clear, so that we can get the needful, pudding and all. That, after all, is why I wrote this note, and not only because the prospect for Christmas pudding is so good. The most impressive phenomenon of the war is the clearance of the sea, assuring our supply of food and our other demands from far. Many Germans in Germany would be glad of an assurance that to-day's food was secured for them, without looking so far as Christmas.

Closing of Pubs. and Clubs. I return to the early closing of public-houses, for this order of the various justices is one of the chief ways that the waging of war is brought home to the citizen in this country, snugly safe for all but for its warriors. 77 of 4 and 5 George V. will now and hereafter require and get attention. The Act applies to "retail licences". only, but is applicable in Great Britain and Ireland, all things unequal con- sidered. No order making the closing hour earlier than nine p.m. is valid without the sanction of the Secretary of State. In Landon 11 p.m. has been fixed instead of 12.30 a.m.; Penny the worse." The nlO on 1 o'clock rule mrusiethale applies to clubs now, and I have had it brought before me when a friend asked for a glass of almost harmless lager beer. The gratifying fact is that all seem to acquiesce without a word just now. How will it be rater On, when some will suggest making the rule permanent, may be a question.

===== 0002601_19141010_art0049.txt | included=true | included =====
Empty Craft and Man's Body Found in the Mersey.

OPEN VERDICT AT INQUEST.

The story of the finding of a boat floating in the middle of the River Mersey at Penketh and containing a gun and only one oar, and of the discovery, almost a fortnight later, of a man's body floating in the water, was related at an inquest conducted by Mr. S. Brighouse at the Red Lion Hotel, Penketh, on Monday morning. The drowned man had been identified as Charles Naylor, a wheelwright, who had lived in lodg- ings at 38, Plumpton-street, Warrington. The first witness called was Elizabeth Naylor, the fourteen-year-old daughter of the deceased, who identified the body as that of her father, who was 50 years of age, and with whom she had lived at the address in Plumpton-street, War- rington. She last saw her father alive at 5.20 on September 19, when he was sober and in good health.

===== 0002601_19141010_art0050.txt | included=true | included =====
CORONER AND THE LANDLADY.

Helen Burthem, the landlady at die Ferry Inn, spoke to the deceased leaving her house at 11 o'clock on the night of September 19th.

The Coroner: He could net have left any later, could he?—Witness: No. Was he sober P—He was quite sober.

What time did he come cannot say. Is there anybody else who knows?—l don't know. I was the only one serving. I could not say how long he was in. How many rooms are there?—Two. When did you first see him before 11 o'clock? —I did not take particular notice. Did you not see him before he left?-oh, yes. What time would it be?—l cannot say. You were the only one serving and there are only two rooms, yet you can't say what time you saw him?—Yes. You are not telling the truth ?—I am. After the witness had left the room the Coroner questioned the police officer. "What did you get out of her?" he asked, to which the officer replied, "I found that she could tell me nothing. The husband was about the place, but he says he did not see the man at all."

John. Smith, an ironworker, 38, Plumpton- street, said the deceased had lodged with him. On the 18th September he asked witness to lend him his small boat, but witness refused because the man was out of work.

===== 0002601_19141010_art0051.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EMPTY BOAT DISCOVERED.

The Coroner: Why did you refuse because he was out of work F--Because I was going away, and I never like to lend it when I am away. Witness, continuing, said that at noon the following day he went down to the Mersey and found his small boat floating in the middle of the water. A gun was lying in the bottom and one of the oars was missing. In reply to further questions the witness said the gun had been taken from his house. De- ceased had no right to use it or to take it. _ _ _ _

- Further evidence showed that when the body was examined by the police no marks of vio- lence were visible. A search of the deceased's clothing brought to light 27 pawn tickets and fourpenoe in copper. An open verdict was returned, there being insufficient evidence to show how deceased got into the water.

A Warrington Opinion.

Mrs. C. Porter, of M, Marbury-street, Latch- ford, Warrington, says‘ "I caught a severe cold on my kidneys about six —months ago, and as a result I began to suffer with fearful stabbing pains in the small of my back. The attacks would come on very suddenly, and nearly brought me to the ground at times. It felt as if I was being stabbed with knives. "When I got to bed the pain was so bad that I could not turn over. I got up in the mornings feeling as tired as if I had just done a day's work. I was sleepy and listless all the time— all my energy seemed to leave me.

My back did not ache so much, and as I persevered with the Pills the pains left me altogether, and soon I was quite well again. I heartily praise Doan's Pills, for they have done me a power of good. BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

ANOTHER DISAPPOINTMENT.

The fortnightly meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians was held on Wednesday morning, Mr. A. J. Jolley presiding.

"TAXING THE WORKERS."

The Guardians were asked by the Pontefract Union to support the following resolution: " That in the opinion of this meeting the Gov- ernment of this country ought to at once make adequate provision for all persons who are in- jured whilst doing service for the country in the war and in case of death for their dependents; and further recommend that the money re- quired for this purpose should be raised by a special income tax to be levied on all incomes exceeding 30s. per week." Mr. McKinley: I move it be deferred. The Chairman: Let it lie on the table. This course was adopted. PROBATIONER NURSES. It was decided, on the proposition of Mr. W. C. Brooks, that Miss Daisy Lilian Nickson and Miss Annie Frances Finn be appointed proba- tioner nttrses at a salary of .29 per annum, ris- ing by annual increments of £3 to a maximum of Xl5 per annum, together with emoluments valued at £4O per annum.

WARRINGTON RURAL DISTRICT

COUNCIL.

The Call for Next Half-Year.

The monthly meeting of the Warrington Rural District Council was held at Bewsey Chambers on Wednesday afternoon, Councillor William Furness presiding.

===== 0002601_19141010_art0057.txt | included=true | included =====
HALF-YEAR'S CALL.

The Clerk (Mr. J. C. Sutton) reported that the treasurer's book was before the Council and showed a balance of £2,339 13s. 4d. in favour of the Council, compared with £4,754 at Michael- mas, 1912, and £3,072 ss. 6d. at Michaelmas, 1913. Calls for general expenses at October, 1912 and 1913, have been made at the rate of sd. in the Xon "the asses.sable values. The former annual progressive values have not been experienced, the totals having fallen from £95,509 at September, 1912, to £93,257 for the half-year just expired. All calls for general and special expenses have been paid, notwithstand- ing the increase of outlay and the decrease of balances and values, it is estimated that a call of sd. in the X to meet general expenses will suffice for the half-year ending 31st March next. If a sd. call was made for the next half- year, it would need 6d. for the following half- year.

It was ultimately decided that a call of sd. in the should be made.

SMALLPDX TREATMENT.

The Clerk stated that he had been in com- munication with the Town Clerk of Warrington and had received a reply, dated 23rd September, which set forth that while the Warrington 'Health Committee were not in a position to guarantee the taking of cases from the Rural District Council's area, it could be taken for granted that whatever assistance could be given would be rendered. A "STINKING BROOK."

The Rev. A. M. Mitchell asked whether any- thing could be done with the "stinking brook" at Burtonwood. It was stinking away most gloriously, because of the fine weather. The matter had now been under consideration for years. The Chairman said that the matter had been debated previously, and—he believed it was the Parish Council who had the remedy. Councillor Knowles said the brewer near by had offered assistance. Rev. Mitchell said pressure must be brought upon the Coutty Council. It was monstrous that the thing should be continually deferred. The Medical Officer (Dr. Sephton) said the brook was a common sewer, and the township should remedy the nuisance. Rev. Mitchell; I don't care whether it is a common &,wer or an uncommon sewer, it is a stinking affair. (Laughter.) Rev. Mitchell was unable to obtain a seconder.

BRITISH TRADE AND THE WAR.

The Trade We Win We Must fold , Permanently.

I have heard a numbe f which British firms have m thepastmZste: se-in _ o concrete _ cured valuable orders which had before gone to world between Germany; but there is all the difference in the d bet;en substituting our goods for Ger- man goods now, and permanently displacing the German trade. While Germany is out of the running, it is .ohvious that buyers at home and buyers abroad will accept substitutes which are not quite what they want. When the war is over, however, and Germany gets under way again, there will be a very different tale to tell, unless the British trader can equal or surpass the German production. Now there are all sorts of things, from boots to toys, and from cheap lace collars to buttons of sorts, for which the market is hungry, because the German sup- ply has ceased, and it is astonishing to find in how many unsuspected departments of industry the German has made a big place for himself. Buyers are eagerly searching for manufacturers who will undertake to produce substitutes. It is for the British manufacturer not only to fill the torders ge tnow,a pe rhmuat nteon tfi ho ledm ixi tuhc eh vf a sa rhi oi oung trades. To use a time-honoured commercial phrase. It is an opportunity which will not occur again.—L. G. Chiozza Money, M.P., in the "Contemporary Review" for October.

THE EXAMINER,

SATURDAY, OCTOBER 10, 1914.

t , •

. i • I

THE EAGLE AND THE LION. We all tremble for Antwerp. Not because we fear that fairest of fair cities will succumb if self-preservation be, humanly speaking, possible. We know that Belgian prowess and resent- ment at bad treatment will oppose the German hordes to the last, and we have an unquenchable faith in the ulti- mate triumph of right. The great guns, however, are at work, and Antwerp, we fear, will have cause to remember this visit from its cultured neighbours. The Belgian cup of sor- row was already full to overflowing, and this further trial will but steel our hearts the more against her persecutor and confirm our determination to exact reparation to the full. That we shall be in a position to do so grows more apparent every day. Nations, specta- tors of this horrible game in which they for the time being can afford to take an outside interest and offer a correspondingly independent verdict, see that there can be only one end. Americans have been among the foremost to condemn the barbari- ties of the Germans ; they are among the first to show that they see the kind of finish the game will have. The editors who produce the papers for our Transatlantic cousins, almost collec- tively, emphasise the opinion that the krestige of Britain "never stood higher : tA,7 rro-

• 1 -ft

===== 0002601_19141010_art0064.txt | included=false | skipped/gap: fewer than 20 non-control characters after cleanup =====
.

===== 0002601_19141010_art0065.txt | included=true | included =====
- • -

than to-day." American financiers think that Germany is beaten, and there is a scramble to sell German credits even at.a loss, while the British exchange continues at its highest point. Whether Antwerp fall or not will make no difference. The Belgians will be made to suffer still further for daring to defend themselves, and, per- haps, as a penalty for having as allies an Army that prevented the. taking of Paris. But the long arm of England and France has already stretched be- yond the Belgian border, and the German moles must, ere long, leave their holes.

Then the Lion and the Eagle will be brought face to face for the final settle- ment. In this connection Mr. H. J. WESTBROOK writes to call our attention to a remarkable passage which applies admirably to the present. situation in the Apocrypha. In the second book of Esdras xi., xii., is an account of the vision of the Eagle and of the Lion. The Lion's address to the Eagle is thus set out:—

"Hear thou, I will talk with thee, and the Highest shall say unto thee, Art not thou it that remainest of the four beasts whoin I made to reign in my world that the end of their times might come through them? And the fourth came, and overcame all the beasts that were past and had power over the world with great fearfulness, and over the whole compass of the earth with much wicked oppression ; and so long dwelt he upon the earth with deceit. For the earth hast thou not judged with truth? For thou hast afflicted the meek, thou hast hurt the peaceable, thou hast loved liars, and destroyed the dwellings of them that brought forth fruit and hast cast down the walls of such as did thee no harm. Therefore is thy wrongful dealing come up unto the Highest and thY pride unto the Mighty. The Highest also -iath looked upon the proud times, and behold they are ended and his abom- inations are fulfilled. And therefore appear no more, thou Eagle, nor thy horrible wings, nor thy wicked feathers, nor thy malicious heads, nor ttlY hurtful claws, nor all thy vain body ; that the earth may be refreshed, and may return, being delivered from thy violence, and that she may hope for the judgment and mercy of Him that made her."

That is what the Lion is saying to the Eagle to-day, with the approval of an outraged world.
