THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, OCTOBER 10, 1914.

Last Week's Memorable Gathering.

===== 0002601_19141010_art0067.txt | included=true | included =====
WHY GREAT BRITAIN MUST WIN.

Mr. A. H. Crosfield, who, along with Mr. Robert Pierpoint and others, had anroppartunity of addressing the great meeting last week, called to promote the formation of our Territorial Reserve Battalion, has written a stirring letter to Captain Max Woods, officer com- manding the Depot, Drill Hall. Mr. Crosfield deals with the many phases of the war, shows why it is a fundamental necessity that the Allies should win, and alludes to the glory that will rest individually upon those who take a part in crushing ,German mili- tarism. We live the letter in full:—

Captain Max Woods, 4th Battalion P.W.V. (South Lancashire Regiment), Drill Hall,

Warrington.

Dear Captain WOods,—l am exceedingly. obliged for your letter of the Ist, but indeed it is I who should be writing to thank you for your. kindness in giving me the opportunity of taking part in our memorable gathering a week ago in the Parr Hall, and to address the men of your Battalion in the Park last Thursday 'morning. When speaking to them I did not attempt to ignore the hardships and dangers that are faced by those who take part in this tremendous European struggle; on the contrary, I think it is far more in keeping with Lancashire tradi- tions and Lancashire character frankly to face the facts.

patching up a half-hearted, inconclusive settle- ment, that we can.uot and dare not count on any Speedy conclusion to the was. There is a ter- rible reckoning sooner or later in store for Ger- many and her rulers. And for that very reason they may refuse to accept the situation, with its bitter, inevitable, overwhelming lmmiliation until they have been not only beaten, but beaten t, their knees. For her, for the Prussian Mili- tary caste, and the Kaiser himself, who between them have led Germany on to her destruction, the final downfall will be tremendous—and it will be deserved.. _ _ _

Lord Rosebery, speaking in Scotland the other day, said it would be years before we knew the inner history of what has led up to this tre- mendous conflagration, and that in the mean- time we should do well if we refrained from saddling the Kaiser personally with more than his share of responsibility. Well, we need not grudge the Kaiser whatever pretext His Majesty may have for pleading extenuating circum- stances. It is reported that the Crown Prince, in alliance with the military caste of Prussia, brought great pressure to bear, and from all we know of them they are not unlikely to have done so. But, when all is said and done, we know very well that the Kaiser, and the Kaiser alone, is in Germany the ultimate repository of power and authority. It is the Kaiser who chooses—or dismisses—his Ministers, and it is his word alone which is decisive for Peace or War. We have had the White Paper by our Foreign Office, and it shows how, acting, of course, in concert with the Prime Minister and the Cabinet, our Foreign Secretary. till the eleventh hour—aye, and until the last second of the last minute of that hour—strove with might and main to preserve the Peace of Europe, and went great lengths in the positive deference be showed to Germany when he would fain have persuaded her to use her influence to avert the war.

===== 0002601_19141010_art0068.txt | included=true | included =====
THE CHANCE OF CENTURIES.

On the other hand, and at the same time, I added that we should not take a gloomy view of the situation and prospect before us, because' —and I emphasised this fact when speaking in the Park—it is well to bear in mind that, after all, of the vast army of those brave sons of Albion who volunteer to serve our country and Empire, and to fight for the mighty causes of such supreme importance to Europe, and indeed to humanity at large, an overwhelming majority will return to their Mother Country when the war is over. What honour and triumph, what a retrospect, what imperishable memoiies they will bring with '-them! What noble traditions to be handed down by their families! Yes, and in our old town by 'Warringtonians, too, in their corporate capacity—through countless generations! Centuries may well pass before such another opportunity presents itself for men of British enterprise and pluck to win their spurs, and crown themselves with the *laurel wreath, accortled'as it 'was in the old days, and will be again to our victors in the field. In normal times it falls to the lot of but a handful of statesmen and chieftains, of men re- nowned in scienco, the arts or literature, to achieve great deeds, and, it may -be, leave im- perishable memories behind them. But now all is changed: the field is open to every man of the British and Irish races, who, fortunate enough to have the opportunity to serve the Empire; has the pluck and the energy to seize it. Our men, one and all of them, will return home heroes—and they'deserve it. The splendid coolness, and courage, and determination with which our men fought their way back from Mons and Cambrai, to the neighbourhood of Paris, and defined the attacks of the overwhelm- ing numbers of the very flower of the German forces brought against them—that achievement, accomplished where we were out-numbered by five or six to one, has already covered our Army with imperishable glory, and will surely go down in history like the Belgian defence of Liege, as an example of supreme courage, worthy to rank with the immortal triumphs of the greatest heroes of antiquity. And if we take our hats off to our Generals, to our Officers, and to our grand Infantry, it is not that we forget the less conspicuous, but no less magnificent service that cur Navy, with ceaseless vigilance night4and day renders to us; its immense service to Europe and the entire British Empire is as vital as invaluable. Nor are we the least unmindful of the dash and courage of our airmen; or the brilliant achieve- ments of our Cavalry, more than a match as they are any day in the week for double, yes, or treble, their number of Germans. Truly, the achievements of our own kith and kin may well fire the enthusiasm of every one of us. Nor less do we admire and rejoice in the splendid achievements of our gallant Allies.

===== 0002601_19141010_art0069.txt | included=true | included =====
NO PATCHED-UP PEACE.

I can well believe that every man serving under you, while he gives others their chance to play their part in these great and glorious achievements, looks forward to doing his own !hare, too. I can well believe that your men are just spoiling for the fight, and, above all things, I congratulate them on the true justice and greatness of the causes which they are serving. If " Thrice is he armed that hath his quarrel just," then great indeed must be our strength; for it would be impossible to exaggerate the justice, the righteousness and the value to the entire civilised world of the causes for which we are fighting. Never in the world's history, have there been such gigantic issues at stake, and it is just for that reason, and just because we can tolerate no half measures, just because neither our Allies nor ourselves will listen to any proposal for

Scenes of activity have been witnessed at the Conservative headquarters, and much good work has been done by these energetic womea.

===== 0002601_19141010_art0072.txt | included=true | included =====
A FALLEN STAR.

—..---.— Since then we have had the Prime Minister's illuminating disclosure at Cardiff; we have had the publications of the Russian Foreign Office; with to-day further light thrown on German methods in Belgium; and we have besides the intensely interesting document written by our Minister at Vienna. Day by day, as the evi- dence accumulates, we see how deliberately Germany had prepared for this war; how the Kaiser, had he chosen to lift his little finger, could have averted it, and yet refused to do so.' Was it too much to ask of him, were it only from his own point of view? Did he not enjoy a personal position enough to satisfy the ambi- tion of any sane mortal? What will'that posi- tion be in twelve months' time? Will the entire history of the world furnish a parallel for such a debacle? _ _

---• Since he, miscalled the Morning Star, _ _

Nor man nor fiend has fall'n so far. Those are not the only lines on Napoleon (written in his life-time) which strike one as vividly and remarkably applicable—in parf, at least—to the present international situation, and just as Britain withstood Bonaparte, so to- day it falls to us to play our part—a part of paramount importance, too, in helping to break the Kaiser's power and draw Germany's teeth. Looking to the future, no one grudges or would deny the German people their opportunity for a useful, unaggressive and peaceful in- dustrial career, but never again, when this war is over, can her neighbours afford to leave her either the oppor- tunity or the power to inflict a seconi time so incalculable and appalling a calamity as that now suffered by the people who dwell along the broad Central Plain of Europe. No, there must be no second Armageddon. Once the Allies have accomplished their stupendous task, then, in- deed, in a most practical sense, "The common-sense of most shall hold the fretful realm in awe," , as a consequence of which, too, Europe will be relieved to an enormous extent from the burden and gigantic expense of 'excessive rivalry in armaments.

NO ALTERNATIVE. One does not speak of the alternative, be- cause, as I said in the Parr Hall, the alternative would be so intolerable as to be inendurable. With its destruction of Democratic Government, of the rights of smaller nationalities, of the sanctity of contract (which, violated in inter- national, would rapidly spread throughout commercial, affairs), with the destruction of the British Empire, with France and Belgium crushed, with the triumph of Blood and Iron, brute force and fraud, over international law and justice, the final victory of the Kaiser would be more than the civilised world could tolerate. And so we are bound to win, were it only because the Allies well know they must go on till they do win. This is no case of "Halloo- ing before we are out of the wood"; on the con- trary, our attitude of mind in Britain is that of men who know the immensity and gravity of the issues, and who are determined that no lack of forethought or sacrifice shall prejudice the final success and triumph of our arms. With cordial greetings and good wishes to yourself, to the officers, and to the men in your Battalion, believe me, dear Captain Woods, very sincerely yours, 41, West Hill, Highgate, N. October 7th, 1914.

A. H. CROSFIELD.

===== 0002601_19141010_art0073.txt | included=true | included =====
lINENOOURAGED ART.

Mr. Mawson and How to tPuderstand

Pictures. A good attendance marked the first of a series of public lantern lectures, which are to be given during the winter session in the Warrington School of Art knder the auspices of the Educa- tion Committee, delivered by the Head Master, Mr. Clarence Mawson, on Tuesday evening. The subject was "How to understand pictures," and was illustrated by a number of excellent slides, covering the masterpieces of a wide range of artists, ancient and modern, of Italian, Dutch, English, Scotch and American extrac- tion. The various points in the pictures, par- ticularly of light, shade and proportion, were carefully explained by the lecturer, who also passed interesting historical comments on many of the illustrations.

The Lecturer said they must not run away with the idea that pictures were the only way in which art expressed itself, for it might find expression in many ways. With regard to art galleries, many people visited these with the idea of seeing a great number of pictures gath- ered together, and they came away without any particular idea of them. Personally, he was rather in sympathy with the suggestion of hav- ing an art gallery containing only half a dozen pictures, set out properly, and occasionally changed for another set. This was much in con- tradistinction to the general idea of a picture gallery, for the net result of a visit nowadays was the idea of a number of good pictures alto- gether, but none of much value in itself. It was, however, well to know how a picture had been painted, in every detail, and it bene- fited them to know, for art was a part of every one of them.

Everyone enjoyed MODERN MONEY-GRABBING TENDENCY. or less. Frequently he saw a crowd of people oyed an artistic production more gathered together before the windows of War- rington antique dealers' shops. Yet how few were willing to take an interest in modern crafts, although men to-day could design furni- ture quite as beautiful! He thought it very singular that, whilst fabulous sums were paid for worm-eaten furniture, there was so little encouragement for modern art. But our pres- ent commercial life tended to drill art out of people, in favour of what might be termed the money-grabbing tendency. Artists were those who saw what others failed to see—arrangement, disposition of light and shade, harmony in colour and proportion, and general idea. To understand a picture, notice should be taken of all these. As to seeing it, a great deal depended upon looking at it long enough, and from the right distance. As pic- tures were the products of the imagination, imagination was also called forth. They should try to see what the artist intended.

===== 0002601_19141010_art0074.txt | included=true | included =====
COUNCILLOR ROBERTS AND NATIONAL SERVICE.

MILITANT ADDRESS.

State's Claim Upon All Her Sons.

A Conscription Act is re-enacted every year, we were told at a meeting held in the Church Institute, Stockton Heath, on Wednesday night, under the auspices of the Stockton Heath branch of the Church of England Men's Society.

NATIONAL SERVICE NOT CONSCRIPTION.

. Councillor Henry Roberts, who presided, said that, perhaps, many of the audience would con- sider that the present time of crisis was hardly a time for discussion, but rather a time for action. There was certainly much to be said for that argument, but if the discussion that even- ing led to action then it would not be in vain. He stood there as one who had been a member of the National Service League for many years past. He belonged to a political party composed very largely of men who did not approve of any- thing which savoured of militarism, and he could assure the gathering that it took him a long time to get rid of his early prejudices and misunderstandings, before he could accept fully and frankly, as he did now, the entire pro- gramme of the National Service League. He had been twitted by his political friends with the suggestion that he was a believer in con- scription. He never had been, and was not now. There was no analogy whatever between conscription and the proposals of the National Service League. He knew that there was a vast amount of misunderstanding as to the pro- posals of the League, but that, to some extent, was owing to the fact that some men who had advocated the League's proposals had, at the same time, avowed themselves as believers in conscription.

===== 0002601_19141010_art0075.txt | included=true | included =====
EVERY MAN'S DUTY.

When considering the question of National Service, they should ask themselves two ques- tions. The first was, "Is it the duty of every man to take his place in the defence of his own country?" (Hear, hear, and cheers.) There had never yet, at any of the meetings he had had the honour of addressing on the subject, been a single individual unpatriotic enough and fool enough to give an answer in the negative. (Ap- plause.) It was, undoubtedly, the duty of every able-bodied man to take his place, if need should arise, in the defence of the country to which he belonged. (Applause.) "Very well," exclaimed Councillor Roberts, "grant me the first proposition and you are bound to grant me the second one, which is: If it is the duty of every man to assist in the flefence of his own country, is it not equally the duty of every man to make himself efficient to carry out that duty? (Cheers.) There is no getting away from it—(cheers)--and these are the two propositions on which the National Ser- vice League was founded. I challenge anybody, either inside this room out of it, to prove that it is not the duty of every man to take a part in the defence of the country and make himself efficient."

The scheme of the National Service League was that when an able-bodied young man reached a certain age he should have to devote a short period in each of three .successive years to military training.

===== 0002601_19141010_art0076.txt | included=true | included =====
SPLENDID RESPONSE TO THE CALL TO

ARMS.

He would like to call their attention to one magnificent feature of the present serious posi- tion. Every one of them had been anaaked at the magnificent response to Lord Kitchener's appeal for soldiers, which had b,en made by the young men of the country duri rg the last few weeks. (Hear, hear.) They all knew that recruits'. came in so fast that the authorities were utterly incapable of dealing with them. They were so overwhelmed with applications that to call a halt for a time they raised the standard of height and chest measurement. The result was that for the moment the excessive demand by the young men for the right and honour of preparing to take their share was slackened, but the moment the standard was reduced he had no doubt that the remaining number of men that might be required would be forthcoming in a very short time. (Hear, hear.)

===== 0002601_19141010_art0077.txt | included=true | included =====
CARRYING ON BUSINESS.

Some of his friends said, "But iFY—oli take all the young fellows away from the works for a period—a period which would be decided by Act of Parliament—how on earth are you going to carry on your buSiness?" "How are we carrying it on now?" asked Councillor Roberts, amid cheers. "We are carrying it on, and we are going to carry it on. (Cheers.) English people very soon accustom themselves to great changes, and there is no doubt that our proposals are 'so perfectly prac- tical that the temporary inconvenience would be overcome in less than one season." He asked them to look on the other side of the case for a moment. They had their Terri-d torials—a splendid body of men they were, too, whose value people were only just beginning to ' appreciate. (Hear, hear.) That force was com- posed of young fellows who had given up volun- tarily their time, leisure and, in ,some cases, money, too, in order to do their duty. Take the case of the firm with whom he was engaged— Crosfield's. (Applause.) There Was often cause to speak of it, and be proud of its men, although he did not allude to it very often, but he would ask if they thought it fair that a works, like that should supply, as it had done for years past, a full company of men to The Territorial Battalion, while lots of other' firms hardly sup- plied a man. He strongly objected to, and pro- tested against, the vicarious performance of per- sonal duty which was shown in so many direc- tions. It was no more the duty of his lads than ' was it of his neighbours' lads to take a part in the defence. It was the equitable duty of the young of the country. (Hear, hear, and cheers.)

QUESTION OF COMPULSION. People said they did not like compulsion. They had to be vaccinated. (Laughter.) Some people objected very strongly to paying the edu- cation rate—(more laughter)—but they. had to pay it. Why? Because it was considered by the Legislature of tha country that those things were for the general good. Well, if the vac- cination was necessary for the general good, and the education rate was necessary for the general good, did they not think that it was infinitely more for the general good that the youth of the country should be trained for the defence of the country? (Cheers.) He sincerely hoped that the misapprehension which existed regarding the National Service League's proposals would be wiped away, so that when the sun of peace began to shine upon them again, as they hoped it would do very soon, they might apply them- selves to making good many weaknesses which had disclosed themselves during the present great times, in order that tht children who came after, and their children's children, could have a heritage of greater security, knowing per- fectly well that no foe would ever be powerful enough to attack them as they were being at- tacked that day. (Loud applause.) CONSCRIPTION ON THE STATUTE BOOK.

Mr. Cooper, of Manchester, said that the ordinary propaganda work of the League had been suspended, but that meeting was arranged long before war was thought of, and it had been considered advisable to go on with it, seeing that the arrangements had been made. Having mentioned the fact that Warrington was the home of the National Service movement, the speaker went on to give an historical resume of the part national armies had played in the story of the world since 700 years B.c. Returning to the League's scheme of National Service, he said that if the proposals matured, every able-bodied man would have to go in train- ing for certain periods. Under conscription every man took his chance by ballot, and if a man with money drew a lot which stated Ile must , train, it was possible for him to pay a less wealthy person to undergo the training in his stead. There was a Conscription Act in this country was was re-enacted year by year, so that conscription was the law of the land. although it had never been necessary to put it into operation.. Probably, however, if the young men had not respianded voluntarily for service in this crisis, the Government would have put it into force.

All Of Them. Sunday School Teacher: And you have no brothers or sisters? Little Edna: No, ma'am. I'm all the chil- dren we've got.

Dearer Milk.

As is now usual at this time of the year, the price of milk has been advanced one halfpenny per quart. The Warrington and District Milk Dealers' Association issued an intimation to that effect last week-end, and the new price will obtain until the Spring.

An Interesting Visit. The Hon. Misses Wilson-Patten visited War- rington last week, and were present at a Paro- chial Mission meeting held in the Patten Hall. The purpose of their visit, say i the Holy Trinity Parish Magazine, " among other things, was to see the hall after it had been beautified in accordance with their instructions. This gives the finishing touch to their beautiful gift to the parish."

. Shire Horse Society Flourishing. At a meeting of the Warrington Shire Horse Society held on Monday at the Griffin Hotel, Sir Gilbert Greenall, who presided, was elected president for the ensuing year, on the motion of Messrs. Richard Billington and W. H. Brad- ford. A satisfactory balance sheet was passed. The officials were re-elected, the Secretary being complimented upon the flourishing state of the society.

Market Prices.

At Warrington Market on Wednesday the following prices ruled :—Wheat, ss. 3d. to ss. 6d. per 701 b.; oats, 3s. 2d. to 3s. 3d. per 451 b.; oat and wheat straw, 20s. per ton. Flour was firm at late prices, viz., 355. per 2801 b. There were indications that oats were being sought after, and the prices were firm. Wheat had advanced id. to 2d. per bushel on the market and re- mained firm.

Wanted: Sunday School Teachers.

In the current issue of the St. Ann's Parish Magazine, the editor makes an appeal for more Sunday school teachers. "We shall be cheered and encouraged," he observes, "if some will offer their services as teachers in our Sunday schools. We need officers to prepare and equip the young for life's work and battles. Let the enthusiasni and self-sacrifice for King and coun- try lead to like volunteering for service in the Lord's work." •

Guardians and Dearer Meat.

At a meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians on Wednesday a letter was read from the contracting butcher, in which he asked the. Guardians to allow him ten per cent. on last month's account—whioh was only a small amount to what he had•lost—as the price of meat had increased considerably.—The Clerk: You have conceded this to other con-

_ tractors.—Mr. Flanagan: The price of meat has advanced considerably.—On the proposition of Mr. Mather, seconded by Mr. Ritchie, the request was acceded to.

Novel Visit to Fever Hospital. At the Borough Police Court on Wednesday morning a twelve-year-old boy was charged with stealing a shirt, value is., from the Warrington Fever Hospital. The evidence showed that the youthful prisoner had been an inmate of the ho_ospital, and on Saturday night he broke into the building and changed the shirt he was wearing for the one he had stolen.—The Chief Constable asked that the case should be ad- journed for a week. The mother said her son wanted to go back to the hospital.—The Bench agreed to the Chief Constable's recommendation.

===== 0002601_19141010_art0082.txt | included=true | included =====
Successful_ Bowling Main.

A highly successful bowling main was decided on the Liberal Club green on Saturday after- noon, when Mr. J. Parr, an old member of the club, triumphed over a number of competitors. In the final he played a veryt good game and de- feated Mr. W. Patrick. Despite the fact that the green has been used a great deal during the season, it was in excellent trim and the players expressed their delight with its condition. Mr. G. Marshall had arranged everything in, satis- factory fashion, and a pleasant afternoon's sport was enjoyed by all.

Psychic Readings and Clairvoyance. Many parents and friends attended ths cele- bration of the Spiritualist Lyceum, which was held in the Druids' Hall On Sunday. Mr. R. Dolphin conducted an open session 4-1 tle after- noon, an interesting lesson being given to the children by Miss Cain, of Chester? who after- wards gave psychic readings and clairvoyance to the adults and children. At the eveniflg. assembly, which was also conducted by Mr. Dolphin,' Miss Cain delivered an address upon "The Future of Spiritualism," and an anthem, "The Lord is my Shepherd," was rendered, Miss Dolphin taking the solo part. Special hymns also were sung, and Maggie Handles recited Longfellow's "Resignation" in excel- lent style. The organ was played by Mr. J. Fostei,•sen.

Coneert for War Fund. As the result of an excellent patriotic pic- ture concert held in the Pavilion, Sankey Green, on Sunday evening,- a sum of over ..tlO will be handed over to the local War Fund. Mr. Foster, the manager, had made admirable arrangements and a splendid programme was disposed of without a hitch. Miss D. Perdue, who also accompanied the singers, opened the concert with a splendid overture, "Battle hymns of the Allies." The Patriotic Choral Party, under the able leadership of Mr. J. E. Hannan, rendered "Comrades in Arms," "Crossing the Plains," and "The Soldier's Farewell," in creditable fashion. Miss Ethel Green was responsible for a pleasing interpre- tation of "Land of my birth," whilst Miss Irtivae and Lily Snelling and Mr. W. A. Warburton also excelled themselves in "Land oflisneo Glory" and "The Soldiers' Song" re The pictures were also very good and included "Heligoland," "With the Troops in Belgium," "Under Two Flags," and "Rosary." The Mayor and Mayoress attended during the evening. They were given a hearty reception, and a complimentary speech was made by his Worship.

Three Millions in Two Months. As will be seen by our advertisement columns, the amount contributed locally to the Prince of Wales' Fund, irrespective of large sums sent direct, is £3,856.

The fund reached the huge sum of £3,000,000 on Monday, a day less than two months of the opening of the list. His Royal Highness has written to, the Press to thank once more the many thousand of generous subscribers who have helped him to achieve this grand result. "I trust," says the Prince, " that the portion of the fund which is to be applied in relief of civil -distress may, as far as possible, flow into productive channels, such as assisting schemes for male and female employment and perhaps industrial training; for it is as repugnant to me as it must be to the recipients that assistance should be distributed only in the form of doles. What men most want is work, and what the young people need is training. The sum which has already been raised is magnificent, and I am confident that the generous British public will continue to do their utmost to alleviate the dis- tress which war inevitably brings in it train." Last night the fund had reached the total of .X 3,104,000.

Opening Our Kinship With the. Animals. The meeting of the new session in connection with the Emmanuel Literary and Debating Society took place on Tuesday in the Institute, linutsford-road, when there was a good attendance. Mr. R. E. O'Callaghan, secre- tary of the Warrington Anti-Vivisection Society, was the lecturer. He spoke on "The Passions of Animals," and, in the course of his remarks, pointed out that in bone, muscle and nerve structure the animals and man were members of one common family, and, further, that in the world of sensation, emotion and thought, animals were separated from man by a differ- ence in degree only. To effectually demon- strate this Mr. O'Callaghan showed a number of rem'arkably well-chosen slides, comparing the animal with the human emotions. Slides, which illustrated love, hate, jealousy, fear, curiosity, expectation, hope, joy and humour, in human and in animal life, were shown. The , lecturer said that his main desire was to open I doors of sympathetic contact and relation be- tween man and his lowly relations, so that from this new standpoint, knowledge and sym- pathy might lead to thexassing of all and every form of cruelty. ,He concluded with an earnest appeal for a developing moral sense, a sense which was outraged by the indefensible cruel- ties of vivisection, were indefensible on moral grounds, useless on scientific grounds, a hind- rance to knowledge, and a blot on the shield of man's faith in the God of Love.

WAR STORIES. WOUNDED AND A PRISONER.

Warrington Man Whom Germans Are Treating Well. _ •

So many stories have found their way to this country concerning the disgraceful treatment which Germany is meting out to the "Tommies" who have fallen into her clutches as prisoners of war that it comes as a welcome relief to hear from the pen of one British warrior, fallen by the way, that he, at least, is being accorded decent treatment. Such intelligence is con- veyed in a letter written by Bombardier G. Brough, of the 23rd Battery of the Royal Field Artillery (attached to the 40th Brigade) to his mother, who resides in Warrington. The letter is postmarked "Gent-Gaud" and states :—" I am wounded in my knee, but it is going on beautifully. . . . I am a German prisoner, but they can't move me to Germany until my leg has got better. But don't upset yourself about that, for the Germans are very good to me and the other chaps who are in the same position as myself. They will give us anything we want. I am, of course in hospital, and there are some Germans in our ward, and when they get anything good brought to them they share it all round. . . . We have grapes, pears, chocolates, and a lot more nice things. You can't beat it! We art living just ,like lords. • • • Reception in France. "I have told you about hospital affairs, and now I will tell you about the reception we got when we landed here tin France). As soon as we arrived the people went mad with excite- ment. . . . They gave us anything you could mention. We were drinking wine made from fruit all day long. They had sandwiches and tea waiting for us, and also eggs. They let us have the run of their orchards and they also gave us handkerchiefs. I never witnessed any- thing like it before in all my life; in fact, they all went mad with joy as soon as we came on the scene. You can guess we came a long way when I tell you that we had a train ride of 17 hours. I dare not say where from. • . . As the trains were standing at the station the French people brought us things, and at every stop there were crowds of people waiting for us." In conclusion, the writer says that he is "doing grand." HORRORS OF THE BATTLEFIELD. Writing to his wife in Warrington, Sergt. Greely, of the 2nd Battalion of the South Lan- cashire Regiment, states that he is in a French hospital suffering from a severe wound received at the Battle of the Aisne, but adds that he is getting on very nicely. "It is terrible," he says, "to see the sights of the battlefield. We have been walking over the Germans' dead . bodies for days. It is awful to see some with their heads off, others with legs off, and the re- mains of some who have been blown to pieces."

===== 0002601_19141010_art0089.txt | included=true | included =====
WITH THE GERMANS.

Private P. Balmer, of the South Lancashire Regiment, and formerly on the permanent staff at drford Blirracks, has sent a letter to rela- tives in Warrington in which he says that he is a prisoner. He writes: We were retiring when I was hit in the right leg, and also in the side. lam now in a Belgian convent, which has been made into a hospital. I was linable to move my leg for a fortnight, but am now getting all right again."

WARRINGTON . WAR INCIDENTS.

George Thomas—Recruit. Amongst the latest recruits in the 4th South Lancashire (Reserve) Battalion Territorials is clieorge Thomas, the Warrington forward. Among the Missing. Mr. Thomas Hankinson, of West-street, has received official notification from the Admiralty that his son, Stoker Hankinson, aged 28, is amongst the men missing from H.M.S. Cressy, one of the three cruisers torpedoed in the North Sea.

Wounded Warrington Warrior. Charles Regan, of 56, Orford-lane, a private in the South Lancashire Regiment, who was wounded in the Battle of the Aisne, is a patient in the Warrington Infirmary. His condition last night was reported to be satisfactory. Boer War " A Picnic." A sergeant of the King's (Liverpool) Regi- ment, who is lying in hospital at Orford Bar- racks, suffering from wounds, told an " Examinor" representative that the South African War was a picnic by comparison with the present struggle on the Continent.

For the Belgian Refugees. Those desirous of giving tangible proof of their sympathy with the unfortunate Belgian refugees are urged to take tickets—price Is. each —for a Belgian Tea, which is to be held in the Parr Hall, from 3 o'clock to 7, on Thursday, October 15th. Music will be provided and the amount realised will be devoted to the local home which is being prepared for the refugees.

Looking After Those Left Behind. Yesterday " The Examiner" representative was informed that the Soldiers' and Sailors' Wives' and Families' Association, whose local office is at the Parr Hall, had relieved about 50 Warrington families during the week. Dur- ing the nine weeks this relief has been in opera- tion, assistance has been afforded in over 1,200 local cases, the total sum paid out amounting to well over ,tl,OOO. There are, of course, fewer new cases needing relief now.

===== 0002601_19141010_art0091.txt | included=true | included =====
DIED HEROES DEATHS.

Warringtonians gilled in the War. 1 The War Office has confirmed the sad news ' that " Bert " Tavlin died from wounds received in the Battle of the Aisne, in which, according to his comrades, he distinguished himself many times. Born at Warrington, the dead warrior joined the South Lancashire Regiment on Sep- tember 26th, 1906. Prior to that he lived with his brother in Orford-street. A son of the late Mr. Joseph Tavlin, who was at one time in busi- ness in Winwick-road, the deceased earned a big local reputation as a boxer, a reputation which was extended when this year he won the cham- pionship of the Southern Command of the Brit- ish Army, In 1909, and again this year, he won the Irish Military Championship, and altogether he was successful in nearly two hundred con- tests. In communicating the news of Bert's I death to his relatives the Rev. Fredk. J. Bing, one of the Army chaplains serving with the Ex- peditionary Force, writes: "lie was given a military burial. . . . He lies in a quiet, peaceful spot. . . . He has given his life for his country and he died a noble death—a sacri- fice of a true and faithful man." "NOBLY PERFORMING HIS DUTY."

"HO was nobly performing his duty under heavy fire from the German artillery, and you may well be proud of his memory." The fore- going is an extract from, a letter written .by Major H. W. Newcome, commanding the 47th Battery of the Royal Field Artillery, to the relatives of Thomas Traynor, a nineteen-year- old Warrington youth, who was serving as a bombardier in the Royal Field Artillery, and who was killed in action at about 11 o'clock on the morning of September 23rd. The Traynor family live at 5, Mill-lane. "The enemy's guns," says the letter convey- ing the sad tidings, " had cut the telephone , wire which connected the observing station with the battery, and as it was most important that it should be mended, your son left the cover of the entrenchment to go and carry out this im- portant duty. A large splinter of a shell struck him in the head and killed him instantly. He was buried by his comrades the same evening in a spot close by. The whole battery mourn his lOss, and feel the greatest sympathy with you and his mother in your bereavement."

OUR BELGIAN VISITORS. The offer of the local committee to provide accommodation at St. Austin's House, kindly lent by Mr. Robert Pierpoint, has been accepted by the London authorities, who say they will send a party of not more than fifteen persons, advising their departure by telegram. That telegram has not yet arrived. The National Committee have accepted the offer made by the family of the lafe Mr. Garnett of the use of "Hall Nook," Penketh, as a home for a number of the Belgian Refugees, who are flocking to this country. Mr. C. E. Parker has actively interested himself in the matter and appeals for assistance in furnish- ing the house and providing provisions for the homeless. The work is being taken up with zeal by many willing helpers in Penketh and Sankey, and the Catholics in Warrington are giving Substantial assistance. "Hall Nook" will accommodate 30 persons, and the cost will be from Bs. to 10s. a week eaeb person.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, OCTOBER 10, 1914.

not appeal to the men within reach of hie voice that it was part of their duty to defend their homes, their country, their kith and kin? Would it not hearten the men in the firing line if they knew that, at home, were large bodies of men prepared to defend their dear ones, their wives and children? He appealed for members of the corps, who, even if they did not have to face the music on the Continent, would have the satisfaction.of knowing that they had done what they could, whilst, at the same time, they would not be any the worse for a little drill and training. They had faith in the gallant Bel- gians; in those splendid fighters, the French; and in their brave Allies, the Russians— (cheers)—but at the same time they must not forget that it was better to be sure than sorry. (Hear, hear.) When peace came—and it would be a glorious peace for this world—there would follow a period of stress; indeed, for the next ten, fifteen, or twenty years there would be no man who would not be poorer than he was when the war started.

===== 0002601_19141010_art0092.txt | included=true | included =====
Days of Stress Ahead.

Each one of them would have to give up something; in fact, he believed that the stress would be so great commercially, industrially, financially and socially, that those who were more fortunately circumstanced would have to be banded together to help those who were poorer to bear their burdens. Which one among them, asked the speaker, in conclusion, would not sooner die than live five minutes under the German yoke? Everyone could do something to avert such a fate. Those who were too old to go and fight could take some share in helping to win the victory for jastice and humanity. He hoped that there was not a man among the audience who would have said to him in the days to come, "You did nothing to help along the victory; you did nothing in the great fight." (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19141010_art0093.txt | included=true | included =====
THE MAYOR'S BENEDICTION.

The MAYOR, who was cordially received, began his brief speech with a personal explanation. He was invited to, and intended to attend, the meeting called by the Conservative party, but when, after being away from Warrington, he came back lie found that some of his friends at Buckley-street had put him down as chairman at an unveiling ceremony, and he felt bound to attend it. Consequently, when he received the invitation for that night's meeting, he felt somewhat puzzled to know whether he would be doing right in attending, and so he asked the permission of some of the leading Conservatives, and they gave it, with the result that there he was. (Laughter and applause.) Ile would like to state publicly how thankful he was, as Mayor of the loyal borough of Warrington, that the Conservative party had taken up that good work of training; it was a noble thought. (Hear, hear.) Contint\ing, his Worship, said that the men of every ward in the town would have rooms close at hand where they could drill in comfort, and the Council offered them the use of the Park, when it was light. The Education Committee were quite willing to lend them the schoolyards to drill in, and the Council had I sanctioned the erection of a temporary rifle range in a convenient place. (Applause.) As to the great happenings on the Continent, he felt sure, in his own mind, that this country was not going to have it all their own way yet awhile, and in view of the time which might come—though they all hoped that it would not —a lot more Englishmen would be compelled to go; the age limit would not stop at thirty-five_ and why should it, seeing the ages of some of the soldiers which Germany was putting into the field? Those who stopped at home ought to be prepared for any emergency that might arise, and it was to that end that the appeal from tha,l platform was being made. By joining up they would receive some drills and would be taught to shoot straight, and if ever the call came they would be ready to take the field at very short notice, instead of having to wait three or six months whilst they were being trained: With that in view he hoped that every man. in War- rington who could do so would put in a few hours at night time and on Saturday afternoons and even on Sundays. (Laughter.) Why not? By doing so, they would not only be doing them- selves bodily good, but would also become more "pally" with one another. After the war was over times would be a bit hard, and the spirit of comradeship which would be fostered by such corps as that they were helping to form that evening would do something to soften those hard times, would help them to bear one an- other's burdens and to sympathise with each other. (Hear, hear.)

The Mayoralty. Referring to the invitation from his brother councillors to take on the Mayoralty for another year, Alderman Peacock said that he felt that he would be very ungrateful to the town and to his colleagues if he did not consent to dis- charge the duties of the office whilst there were strenuous and frightful times ahead. And so he accepted the invitation, and he would endeavour to do his best in the office of Mayor for another twelve months. (Cheers.)

===== 0002601_19141010_art0094.txt | included=true | included =====
STILL MORE MEN WANTED.

Alderman SMETHURST, in moving a vote of thanks to Mr. Parker for presiding, reminded the audience that 400 men were required from Warrington a few days ago to make up the newest battalion of the South Lancashires. Already over three hundred of them were forth- coming, and it was his pleasure to see them marching through the town that day, and a nicer-looking set of young fellows one could not wish to see. (Applause.) Councillor WILKINSON seconded, and it was carried with acclamation. AN OLD SOLDIER STILL FIT.

A brief speech that did not figure on the Chairman's programme was then made from the body of the hall by Mr. T. G. BUCKLEY, of Winwick-road. He was, he said, an old soldier, and he felt fit, and he would like to know, al- though he was 54 years old, why he could not . go out and do "his little bit."' (Applause.) He was the holder of three medals and he served two years and three months in South Africa, under Queen Victoria and King Edward. (Cheers.) He was sure that if the country wanted them, every man who held a medal would be willing to go out to fight the present enemy. As they would not let him go to the front he had sent in his name for the National Reserve, so that he had done something. (Ap- plause.) lie had two sons and a son-in-law serving with the colours. (Cheers.) If old men like himself, the speaker added, were not wanted at the front, why could the authorities not utilise them for garrisoning some of the towns at home? At the same time, however, be did not consider that he was too old for the front.

The meeting closed with the singing of the National Anthem, after which the names of those willing to serve in the Preparatory Corps were taken.

CIIEERS FOR WOUNDED HEROES,

Hundreds of men, and a large number of women, attended a meeting in connection with the Local Training Corps, held in the Parr Hall last evening, when unprecedented scenes or enthusiasm were witnessed. Two wounded soldiers—Sergt. Thompson, of the King's Own Royal Lancasters, and Sergt. J. Walker, of the King's Liverpool Regiment—were given a most hearty reception when they mounted the plat- form, and Alderman Bennett explained that they both came of an old fighting family in Warring- ton. The Mayor, Sir Gilbert Greenall and other leading gentkmen made inspiring speeches, ap- pealing for men for the Corps and for Kitchener's Army.

===== 0002601_19141010_art0096.txt | included=true | included =====
C.L.B. BATTALION IN TRAINING.

The members of the Church Lads' Brigade have answered the call of duty nobly. The organisation's special Battalion of the King's Royal Rifles is now training at Denham, under the command of Colonel Kindersley-Porcher, late of the Coldstream Guards, who has for many years commanded the Soutft-Western Dis- trict Camp of the C.L.8., as well as the Exeter Regiment. That the War Office appreciate the C.L.B. is evident from a communication sent to Field Marshal Lord Grenfell, the governor and commandant, which states: "The Army Coun- cil thoroughly appreciate the good work that has been done and is being done by the Church Lads' Brigade and the excellence of their organ- isation." The whole of the battalion now in training is composed of ex-members, who are putting their early drilling to a useful and patriotic purpose," The question of the forma- tion of a second battalion is under consideration and ex-members wishing to join should send in their names to the Secretary, C.L.B. Headquar- ters, Aldwych House, Catherine-street, Aldwych, London. W.C.

===== 0002601_19141010_art0097.txt | included=true | included =====
Mass Nieeting to Raise a Town Training Corps.

"BETTER BE READY THAN SORRY."

To-day, we are not Liberals, Conservatives, or Labourites, but, first • and foremost, true Britons in every sense of the word, putting aside our individual opinions and joining one with the other to defend the honour of our country. Evidence of this solidarity was to be seen in the "John Crosfield " Memorial Hall on Wednesday evening, when a mass meeting was ' held for the purpose of fully explaining to all interested—and every man of suitable age should be—the scheme of the Warrington Preparatory Training Corps. Those on the platform included well-known gentlemen who take directly opposite views in matters political, but who, in these days of crisis, are at one with their Liberal friends on the one supreme point, viz., that the country needs all her sons in her great fight, and that the organisation of such a corps as that pro- posed, and its strengthening to lite utmost pos- sible limit, is necessary. Various corps have been formed, and all have been amalgamated with the Town's Corps, which is composed of men over 35 years of age. Many patriotic and inspiring speeches were made, and as a result a number of recruits have been enrolled.

Mr. C. E. Parker presided, and there were also present on the platform the Mayor (Alderman Peter Peacock), Aldermen Tinnion and Smethurst, Councillors Wilkinson, Locker, Hawthorn, Lindley, Barton and Gough, Messrs. J. Fulton-Smith, H. Collin, E. Wynne-Jones, W. H. Bellian, E. A. Sanders and H. Hayes, and others. ,•

Mr. E. Wilkes played several selections on the piano, including " National Anthems of the Allies," and other patriotic numbers. Other appropriate items were: "Dear Little Sham- rock," sung by Mr. Walker; "The Veteran Song," by Mr. Barlow; and "The Admiral's Broom" and "Boys of the Old Brigade," by ; Mr. Nurse, &c. There was quite a patriotic air about the hall, for the flags of the Allies had been arranged in tasteful fashion over the platform, the front of which was also draped with the Red, White and Blue of England.

THE GREAT TIMES IN WHICH WE LIVE. "We are living in great times, and it is a grand thing to be an Englishman—a Britisher." Such was the CTIAIRMAN'S opening sentence. Continuing a striking and inspiriting speech, he said the country was in for great sacrifices, and after the war was ended they would pro- bably be worse off than they were before. Many of the homes in the country would be bereft of the breadwinner; there would be a great deal of destruction of the results of effort and toil. Yet, in spite of those things, he was glad to be living in such times—(hear, hear)—because they were fighting for what they believed to be a worthy cause. (Hear, hear.) We had not bared our arms from any lust of territory, but in order to fight for what we believed to be most precious in civilisation—the honour of our plighted word and on behalf of weaker nation- alities. (Hear, hear.) That being so, any sac- rifices :we could make, or any work we pould do, should be freely made and done in this time of peril. (Hear, hear.) "We are glad we are Englishmen," continued Mr. Parker. "We are very independent; we split into parties and sects, and, in ordinary times, we fight for our opinions with zeal and earnestness, and, when this is over, I suppose we shall resume our local fighting. If this were an ordinary occasion I should tell Mr. Bellian that his politics were absolutely rotten. (Laugh- ter.) Probably he would be ready to reply to me, 'Parker, your politics are not only rotten, but absolutely stinking.' (Laughter.) We are glad, in this time of need, to be able to sink all these differences—and it is our duty to do so— and whether Conservative, Liberal, Labour, or Irish, whether we belong to this sect or that party, we put our different opinions in the back- ground for the time being. (Hear, hear.)' We stand as a nation absolutely united and deter- mined to see this thing through to the finish." (Applause.)

The Empire-wide Response. /

Further illustrating the solidarity of the Empire, Mr. Parker said he was glad to think that not only had the people of England, Scot- land, Ireland and Wales responded to the call, but that the Colonies across the seas were send- ing their help and sympathy'. (Hear, hear.)

Those children of Britain's island breed, To whom the mother in her need, In tinies of, stress doth call.

In that sympathy and help he felt sure that they could place great confidence and trust. (Hear, hear.)

Whom should we trust, if not our kin? And whom account so near in natural bonds as these? Born of our Mother England's mighty womb, Nursed on our Mother England's mighty knees, And lulled as we were lulled in glory and gloom, With cradle song of her piotecting seas. They knew how those children were answering the mother's call. Away in the Far West, thousands of miles across the seas, they had heard and were answering the call of the blood. In the lumber and mining camps of the wild Rockies they had also heard and were answer- ing the call. The cowboys from the ranches around Calgary were sending theif contingents. Men were also coming from the wheat fields of Manitoba and Winnipeg, and from the great cities of Toronto, Montreal and Quebec. They were coming from Australia and New Zealand, and Boers and, Britons together had sailed from South Africa. (Applause.) And then they must not forget that magnificent contingent from India, which had been sent to Marseilles. He was only sorry that the voyage had put the horses out of gear, and that they had to stay a week or so in the port of landing to get them into form. Not only were men coming from the tribes and provinces ef India, but the Indian princes were proving by their gifts of service, money and stores that they recognised the rule of England as just and righteous. (Applause.) North, south, east and west people had heard the call of the Motherland, and had answered to the call of the blood. With such assistance and determination it seemed almost incredible that the nation should go under. There was one characteristic which had been strikingly manifested by those in authority in political and military spheres, and that was that they seemed determined from the beginning of the war to take no Chalices. (Hear, hear.)

had acted just a few days after the declaration of • war in initiating the Training Corps, Mr. Bellian said they were prompted only by the fact that they had the welfare of the nation at heart. Probably the idea• was in the Mayor's mind, and he had not the slightest doubt that H they had not moved in the matter, then his Worship would have done so. (Hear, hear.) They did not want men in the corps who were within the age limit to join Kitchener's Army. The age limit for the corps had been raised to 50, (A Voice: The old Bucks !") He appealed to every man who was able and entitled to do so to join the corps, thereby showing that he was ready to go through a course of instruction I to make him fitted, so that if catastrophe did come they would be ready. (Hear, hear.) What was the corps for? It was to prepare them so that if the necessity did arise they would be able to relieve those who had gone to the front.

===== 0002601_19141010_art0098.txt | included=true | included =====
Taking No Chances.

Whilst it was very unlikely that the Army would be defeated in France or the Navy crip- pled, or that this country would be called upon to sustain an invasion, they were taking no chances, and in Warrington, as in many other towns there was a strong feeling that in any event of that kind they should be ready in the old town. (Hear, hear.) That brought them to- the object of the meeting—the formation of a Preparatory Training Corps. His wife had called that an absurd title, and suggested "The Bucks." (Laughter.) He assured them that if he could be of assistance to them he would be proud to make one of the corps. (Loud ap- plause.) WAR HAS DONE THE NATION GOOD. Mr. BELLIAN explained the whole scheme, and in the course of an eloquent appeal, said they were all agreed that if anything had done good for the nation it was the present war. It had re-formed friendships that had been broken for a considerable time, and eventually it would better the spirit of the whole nation. (Hear, hear.) He believ&l that if they could only analyse the feelings of the Kaiser at the present time, they would find that he was thinking that one of the greatest mistakes he made when he• declared war against the world was the fact that he thought England was not in a position to amalgamate the forces within her realm which were antagonistic to each other at that time. They had shown the Kaiser and the world at large that they could sink all party differences and stand united for the country of which they were proud to be members. After speaking of the promptitude with which people

===== 0002601_19141010_art0099.txt | included=true | included =====
If the Worst Comes.

If the worst came to the worst they would sell the last drop of blood as dearly as it was possible to sell it. If the Government com- pelled everyone of a certain age to go to the front, it would give those who formed that corps an opportunity of relieving the other men by taking over the home defences of the coun- try. There was nothing to lose but a great deal to gain by joining the corps. It was better to be ready than sorry, and whilst it was not in- tended that any of the corps should go to the front, he felt sure that if the necessity did arise everyone in the room would be prepared to go. (Hear, hear.) It was possible that that would never come about, but they did not know, and he would feel that he had done one little stroke of business for the country in this time of crisis if he were able to persuade anyone to join the corps. The Germans were not yet out of France and Belgiqm. Was not that a serious position? The Allies were not advancing at any great rate, and we did not know whether they might have to retreat. They all prayed that it might not be so, but there was the neces- sity of being ready, and he could assure them the drill would do them no harm, but would make them feel, after they had had the train- ing, that they were a lot better for it. (Ap- plause.)

===== 0002601_19141010_art0100.txt | included=true | included =====
FITTED TO DEFEND HEARTH AND HOME. _ . .

Mr. HENRY COLLlN:w—h.o—w.as accorded a hearty reception, said that if he could do anything to assist the Liberal party to enrol men in the pro- posed corps he would be only too glad. He had many times had the pleasure of speaking to those whom he was glad to call his Liberal friends—(applause)—but never before on such a subject as that before them that evening, or in that fine ball, upon the possession of which he congratulated the party. He would like to add his appeal to those voiced by previous , speakers, and trusted that as a result of that meeting many men would come. forward and join the Warrington and District Preparatory Training Corps, and they need not bring their birth certificates with them when they gave in their names. (Laughter.) Rooms would be found for them to drill in, and the town had been divided up into wards in order to facilitate the men who came forward. -Let those who were able give a few hours' service; it would do them much more good than haggling over a matter they did not know much about. (Laugh- ter and applause.). To those who might be past the age of fifty his message was: "If you come to do three or four drills you will imagine that you are not as old as you are, and when you have made yourselves efficient you will be pre- pared and fitted to defend, your hearths and homes should occasion- require it None of those listening to him could tell whether or not they might be called upon to do their bit! And if the need for calling upon them should arise, it was better that they should be pre- pared than unprepared. He would like to take that public opportunity of thanking the War- rington manufacturers for placing rooms at the disposal of the projected corps. If the manu- facturers were prepared to do their share, surely the men were! (Applause.) If they could see their way to make that corps big and success- ful, Warrington would be proud of them. (Ap- plause.) It had to be remembered that in en- rolling members of that corps they must not take men who came within the age limit of Kitchener's Army. There were no politics about the Preparatory Training Corps, as would be seen from the fact that the head of the movement was the Mayor, whilst the Chairman of the Executive was Mr. Fulton-Smith. (Ap- plause.) Mr. Wilkinson was the honorary treasurer, and Mr. Percy Bennett the honorary secretary. If affairs on the Continent went against this nation—and they could not foretell, for the Allies were up against a mighty host; against a man who could throw a huge number of men into the field—the Government would want more men, and they would get them. (Cheers.) There could be no question about that. Every man in the country, no matter what his age, was prepared to do his share, if the authorities would allow him. (Cheers.)

FREEING "TOMMY" FROM ANXIETY. Mr. FULTON-SMITH, who was introduced to the meeting as "the Chairman of the Bucks," said that it was up to those who had to stay at home to assure those who had gone to the front that, to the last drop of their blood, they would defend the homes from which the soldiers had had to go, so thht they need have no anxiety on that score whilst they were away. (Hear, hear.) Continuing, the speaker said that the people of this country were determined that she must be predominant in the affairs of the nations, as she had been in the past, and that the benign rule which it had been the privilege of the British people to enjoy for generations past should be upheld. Unless they were pre- pared to stand by that their patriotism was somewhat at fault. "I heard the mother say to one son the other day," the speaker pro- ceeded, speaking under stress of great emotion, " I am so sorry you didn't join before. Soldier- ing is evidently your forte, and I am glad you are going to the front! '" tCheers.) They wanted, he continued, to stir every young man in Warrington to come forward to give his ser- vices, and, if need be, his life, to maintain the honour and glory of the Empire, and the pride ,of his own home. (Renewed cheers.) He I attended his first drill on Monday, and he had not acquired the goose-step yet, but if everyone was as anxious as he was it would be a step in the right direction, and a step from which bene- fits would accrue, physically, morally and patriotically. The speaker went on to refer to the atrocious brutality which seemed to per- meate the German nation at the present time, and added, amid applause, that the sooner that sort of thing was stopped the better it would be. (Hear, hear.) In conclusion, the speaker hoped that the appeal made that night would have the desired effect, and paid a tribute to the Mayor for the splendid work he had d, in connection with the movement.

PATRIOTIC FOOTBALLERS

Desert Play Field for THE Field.

All the playing members of the Orford St. John's Association Football Club, with the exception of two, who had the most satiSfac- tory reason for abstaining, and the committee have decided to abandon football for this sea- son and to join the Army. The club was in the first division of the Warrington League, and also in the Widnes League, and the deci- sion affects the men in both teams. The men who have thus shown what footballers can do when the interest of their King and country is at stake, have been warmly congratulated by their Chairman, Mr. Llarry Ashton, by .Widnes friends, and many others. Orford St. John's have had a most successful career, having been champions of the League for the last four or five years, and having won the trophiei in the Medal Competition nine times. In fact, they have lost only six matches in five seasons. They are all under 21 years of age, in good form, and are hopeful of being as successful against the Kaiser's conscripts as they have been against all opponents on the football field. .

They lia\la joined various 'units. For in- stance, the Secretary, Mr. John Smith, 21, Norris-street, has joined the Army Service Corps, while the Treasurer, Mr. George Tom- linson, has joined the Territorials. So completely have the club gone over to the Army that the field has been given up and the goal-posts have been sold.

THOSE " TONLMY'S " LEFT BEHIND. Council Advocate Proper Provision for Soldiers' Dependents.

The General Purposes Committee submitted to the,Town Council, for their consideration at their monthly meeting on Tuesday evening, the following minute: "It was resolved, on the recommendation of the Executive Committee of the Local Relief Committee, that the following resolution be approved and acted upon: That representation be made to the Government that the scale of allowances at present in force for the wives and dependents of soldiers, reservists and Territorials, ifo inadequate, owing to the increased cost of living, and that, in the opinion of this committee, those fighting for the country should be properly provided for by the State, as in the case of the Navy.' " Arising out of the minute, Councillor Archer said that an explanation was due to the persona who had charge of the arrangements at Parr 'Hall. He had had brought to his notice certain cases and he wished, in the best interests of those working at the hall, to say that he in- vestigated thew cases, and, in justice to the people who had been working so hard, that he found on examination and inquiry that there was no justification for what had taken place. He felt constrained to pay a tribute to the Relief Committee for the work they had done. Relief Work Carefully Done.

Aldermi:n-Benneii said that he was immensely impressed by the great care which was taken in this important relief work, and by the close, personal attention that was given to every case. He thought the whole of the people responsible for the organisation of the department were de- serving of best thank. Councillor Chrimes said that the whole of the cases brought before the Relief Committee had been gone into very thoroughly, and it had been proved that the statement which he had made at a previous meeting was quite true; but the lady in question had misunderstood the person who had spoken to her, and it was not an offi- cial who said it. He had traced the case, and was quite convinced that no harm was intended. The Mayor: You are all out of order, but I thought I would let you go on. Councillor Dictenson: I was only going to say— The Mayor: You, too, are out of order. (Laughter.) The minute was thea formally confirmed.

BLUE TUNIC EXCHANGED FOR KHAKI.

According to a report sUbmitted to the Town Council on Tuesday evening, eight Warrington police constables have been called up on the Army Reserve, two of them being married, and the other six single. Pending a further report from the Chief the application of P.C. Huzzey, who has joined the colours, for the return of rateable deductions from his pay, on account of the pension fund, was deferred.

WHERE YOU MAY LEARN TO SHOOT.

Public Rifle Range to be ProNlded.

When the minutes of the Town Hall, Parks and Baths Committee were submitted for ap- proval to the Town Council at their monthly meeting on Tuesday evening, Alderman James Evans said he would like some little explanation with regard to the fol- lowing item: "Instructions were given for the laying out of a temporary rifle range for the use of the public on the land at the rear of Garren House." In asking for the explana- tion, the speaker said, he would first of all like to make it clear that he did not wish to offer any opposition to a rifle range. But he had been talking the matter over with the Medical Officer I of Health and he would like to know whether the spot where the firing was to take place from was just near the Dispensary that had just been put up. If so, he would move that the minute be referred back to the committee. He did not know how long it was proposed to continue to use the suggested range, or whether it was merely intended that 'practice should take place on it during the time of recruiting and the period of the war. The Mayor (Alderman Peacock): That is so!

Aldermanic Doubts.

Alderman Evans: I can't help thinking that it will be a _very serious matter, because in this Dispensary consumptive patients will have to be examined from time to time, and if this firing is to take place without any restriction as to hours, it may have an injurious effect upon them. It would be very awkward for the Medical Officer of Health to have to examine minutely the consumptives who came before him whilst the firing of rifles was going on just underneath his window; in fact, that portion of his work could not very well be carried out. He (the speaker) would like an explanation from the chairman of the committee as to the pre- cise locality of the range, &c., as it might alter his attitude considerably. Councillor Dickenson said that he was given to understand that the range would only be used during the period of the war, and when that was ended it would be discontinued. That was all the explanation he could give. Range Will Not Disturb the Sick. Alderman Smethurst explained that one side of the wall of the baths would be used RS a sup- port for the range and that a temporary cover would be put over it. The range would be far away from the office of the Medical Officer of Health. The Mayor: As a matter of fact the range will only be put up in a place that will suit the Medical Officer. Councillor Pemberton: The Medical Officer won't be examining consumptive patients all day, surely, but only for a short time, and could not firing be stopped just for that period? Alderman Bennett, appealing to the members not to take any short-sighted view of the matter,

opined that every facility should be afforded to those who desired to benefit their country. A large number of citizens were joining together in order to acquire some military training, and, naturally, facilities were desired for as many as possible. They all hoped that the proposed range would only be a temporary necessity. The committee's recommendation was adopted, Alderman James Evans expressing his satisfac- tion with the Mayor's assurance.

WARRINGTONIANS FOR THE WAR.

This week's recruiting at Warrington has totalled about 100 from the Cairo-street, Bea- mont and Police stations, whilst that of the Drill Hall has accounted for 50. Altogether 266 have recruited at the Drill Hall since it was opened for the purpose.

ARMY SERVICE CORPS.

• Appeals to the manhood of the town are still being made on behalf of the West Lancashire Division Transport and. Supply Column. The original local company has bravely volunteered for active service, so that those who now listen to the call will form a reserve company. Sad- dlers, farriers and motor and horse owners are particularly requested to hand in their names, and those amongst these classes, who view the call with favour, should report themselves to a recruiting officer, who will attend at the headquarters in Degh-street each evening be- tween seven and nine o'clock.

CROSFIELD'S AMBULANCE MEN FOR THE FRONT. Sergeant J. E. Newns, of the Crosfield section of the St. John Ambulance Brigade, writing to Superintendent Rose, from Devonport Naval Barracks, details how the ambulance men have carried out their duties at that place, but adds: "As you know, this has not been suffi- cient for us, as it is our ambition to be nearer the front We have at last got our wish, and we are to leave for the Continent- -120 of us. We are to go to Dover, and from there embark to—Somewhere! I cannot tell you how pleased we all feel at this news: • • - I am like the rest of the boys—cannot write the news fast enough."

EARLY LIORIIING FATALITY.

Man Falls into River at Bank Quay

Wharf.

INQUEST STORY.

The adjourned inquest on the body of James Keefe (38), 55, Howard-street, Warrington, was resumed at the Stag Hotel, Lower Walton, on Tuesday afternoon. Mr. E. Aubrey White con- ducted the inquiry and others present were Mr. Brothers (lI.M. Inspector of Factories), Mr. W. L. Crawford (who represented Messrs. J. Cros- field and Sons, Ltd.), and Mr. Hodgson (secretary of the local branch of the Dockers' Union). -

From the previous evidence it appeared' that deceased was working on an empty boat at Bank Quay Wharf at 1.30 on the morning of October Ist, when he fell overboard. It was very foggy at the time. William Whitfield, foreman of the gang, con- ' tinning his evidence, said he was engaged on a loaded boat which was attached to the empty one by a rope. He was heaving in the loaded boat, when he heard a man cry "Billy! There's a man in the river." The Coroner: Who was it that shouted?—A man_ named Kelly.

When witness heard the shout he ran towards the stern of the boat and shouted for the life- buoy. There was a life-buoy on the jetty, but it was not thrown out into the river. The Coroner: Why P—Because none of .my own men were there.

Witness threw a rope in the direction of where the deceased had gone down, in the hope of his grasping it. Witness climbed up the jetty, but a man named Burrows, who had heard the cry for a life-buoy had secured a boat and was searching for the man. This boat would be on the scene in about 20 seconds.

The Coroner: It was a foggy morning?—Yes,

Could you see very far from the boat?—A cer- tain distance; only a very few yards. The body was found about 6.30 the same morn- ing. Questioned by the Inspector, witness said that it was not necessary to have lights on the boat. There were incandescent electric lights on the shore, and these would be about the length of the boat away. Was there anything between the point where the deceased fell in and the shore?—Yes; two boats. .

He would fall between the two boats?--"Yes, sir. _ _

So that whatever means of support had been thrown out would not have been able to get to him P—There would be fio chance. Edward Kelly, wiredrawer, IQ, Ernest-street, Warrington, 'who was employed as a casual labourer by Messrs. Crosfield on the Bank Quay Wharf, could not say whether Keefe was able to swim. Witness was working on the full boat and from this to the empty one on which the man was standing was a rope. Keefe had hold of one end of the rope and witness held the other end, until Keefe shouted to him to put the rope round the " stump" on the boat. Wit- ness did that and the next thing he heard was a splash in the water. He immediately raised the alarm. He did see the man in the water, but it was only a "very few seconds" afterwards that he disappeared from sight owing to the fog. Keefe must have'fastened his end of the rope to the empty boat before he fell in. In answer to the Inspector witness said the foreman rushed down towards the stern imme- diately the shout was raised and threw a rope in the water. He estimated that he could not see beyond three yards owing to the fog. Arthur Yates, dock labourer, 64, Garibaldi- street, gave evidence of recovering the body at 6.30 the same morning. The Coroner, in summing up, said it appeared to have been a very foggy morning and so far as they could see the work had been carried out under ordinary normal conditions. The place seemed to be well lighted, as well as could be expected in the circumstances, and there was no suggestion of anything but that the man met his death accidentally falling into the water. The jury returned a verdict of "Accidental death."

THE NEED OF FIRE PROTECTION.

In the present crisis the question of fire pro- tection is important, not only with regard to arsenals and other national establishments, but also country mansions, with their countless treasures and works of art. The Earl of Londesborough, in conjunction with the British Fire Prevention Committee, is organising a Special Fire Service Force, for emergency ser- vice. The Earl maintains a steam fire engine and an efficient brigade at his seat, Blankney Hall, Lincolnshire. Weeting Hall, the seat of Mrs. Hall, on the borders of Norfolk and Suf- folk, is another historic mansion now protected by an up-to-date motor fire engine, supplied by Messrs. Nierryweather and Sons, London.
