JOTTINGS BY AN OLD JOURNALIST.

Lymm Item. • The Rev. G. B. Thurston's retirement from the Lymm Urban District Council was attended by a disagreeable incident, but, if to set up such a comparison be not of the nature of an anti- climax, there is an old Norse legend which . friends and admirers of the Rector may take to their comfort. Skrymir, arming himself with a big hammer, strikes therewith the sleeping Thor a blow on the forehead. Thor opens his eyes, passes his hand over the place which has been hit, and says, "Did a leaf fall?"

Why Not More Centenarians? The death of a Penketh lady in her 93rd year was last week recorded in these columns, and as I read the particulars I was reminded of an article in which some time ago Sir James Crich- ton-Browne contended that, so far from having to fall out of employment with the appearance of grey hair, no man need be too old at twice forty; in fact, that everyone might retain his ' full abilities until he had made up the century. I will not trouble the reader with the detailed, argument; it may suffice just to state that the secret of obtaining those additional years was held to lie in a carefully-nurtured and educated childhood, attendance thereafter to the simple laws of health, attention to hygiene, avoidance of vice, and judicious dieting. Moreover, a great deal of premature decline in force and energy being declared to be due to over-use of the brain and nervous system, we must not merely bear in mind that whatever tends to diminish bodily disease is conducive to longev- ity, but in our endeavour to promote it we must have regard also to mental hygiene.

Something Like a Pike. As I read of Mr. Efounfield's preference for pike fishing, I was reminded of the Irishman's yarn. "How big do the pike really run in the lough?" the boatman was asked, and the reply was, "Big enough to swallow a tailor." The fisherman expressing doubt, the following was quoted as precedent : " When Cassidy, the tailor, quarrelled wid his wife, an' told her to go to the dhivil, she up and said 'No, she'd lived long enough wid him already.' Then Cassidy was that mad he lift the house, an' never a hair av him was seen ag'iti, until Red Shemus caught the big pike wid the buckle inside av him. An' the Widdy Cassidy recognised the buckle as belonging to Cassidy's belt, and bought the pike av Red Shemus, an' waked it—a most illegant wake she gave it—an' wanted the praste to read over it. But his riverence wouldn't do ut; so the Widdy buried the pike in the corner of her , patch widout benefit of clargy, an' ordered an illegant tombstone for it, wid 'Pray for the soul

_ of James Cassidy in his passage through the Pike to Glory' on it, an' married Phelim, the fiddler, on the evidence of the buckle." There's something to make Mr. Mounfield's mouth water—and in due course to send him to Ireland again on a pike fishing expedition, eh?

"A Cockney Shakespeare." So Fitzgerald described Dickens, and it is a felicitous phrase. It recurred to my mind as I read the summary of Mr. Stanley C. Tones' lecturotte on "Dickens and social reform." It has been truly observed of the master that he never satirises unless he has an abuse to attack. Thus his exposure of the scandalous Yorkshire schools brought him 'threats of legal proceed- ings from more than one quarter. Then what of his withering denunciations of the Court of Chancery and the Circumlocution Office? Cer- tainly, he it was who broke the bad old tyranny of the workhouse. Granted that he gives many hostages to criticism, if it chose to enforce its claim, the very first man to put that into words hastened to admit that no writer since Shakes- peare has enriched our circle of acquaintance with such an abundance of entertaining people. The sayings of some of these have given us fresh words and phrases, and many of 'them have from my youth been as real to myself as per- sonal acquaintances. So, surely, most of my readers will say as they think of Sam Weller, Mrs. Gamp, Bumble, Sikes, Crummles, Dolly Varden, Bunsby, and the rest of the immortal host.

Wasted Effort.

"The Gunmakers of Moscow" was the title of the principal item at the Star 'Cinema for the former half of the present week, and, as I watched some recruits being initiated into the mysteries of target practice, the combination somehow reminded me of a story of an officious sergeant who, while so engaged a while ago, took the gun from the hands of the first man in the line and said: "Er—this is a rifle, you know. This is the barrel, this is the stock, and this is the magazine. You put the clip of cartridges in here. These little things on the barrel are the sights. You look along them when you take aim. Then you fire by touching this little thing, which is the trigger. By the way, what was your previous occupation?" "Who, me, sir?" said the recruit. "Why, I was a gunsmith." The White Plague.

"The fresh air idea has been exaggerated," said a certain doctor to me not long ago. While I could not in the least agree, I do not think there is really as much to be hoped from sana- torium treatment as sanguine supporters have supposed. The matron of such an institution in Yorkshire told me years ago that only those patients who were in the incipient stage bene- fited, and many of these got rheumatism; and that when they returned to the old conditions at home consumptive symptoms frequently re- curred. The truth of the matter is that in the course of the last half-century—of course, with- out sanatorium treatment—the mortality from phthisis has fallen nearly 60 per cent, as the result of gradually improved sanitation which continues at an ever-accelerating rate. ,How- ever, we are inevitably committed to increased sanatorium accommodation, and, after all, one is free to admit that this, together with in- validity insurance, will contribute to that early treatment of the disease from which alone bene- fit is derivable. Above all, outgoing convales- cents must be carefully instructed as to con- tinued self-treatment at home. Most emphatic- ally, when all is said and done, prevention is ever so much better than cure; and the avoid- ance of what is still sufficiently ierious to be termed the white plague is a more simple matter than many have yet realised.

The Birth-rate. In view of the fact that during the last 30 years the birth-rate in these islands has declined by one-third, Warrington is entitled to indulge in a prolonged crow of triumph over the pretty substantial increase in the local birth-rate last week as compared with the corresponding period twelve months ago. We were all getting so down in the mouth about the state of things in this respect. A Scottish contemporary lately ob- served that if the decrease continued 1" it is evident that at the end of the century we shall have to revert to the cld practice of annexing our babies from the parsley beds." A. medical officer commented that young men in the middle and upper middle" classes of society selfishly postpone marriage so that they /may live in a more luxarions manner, have more amusements, and have more money To Spend on holidays. They like the pleasur it f ladies' society, but. e o they forego -s natural corollary—marriage. Ab; but at nurn are now rallying to-the colours for the defence of England, home and beauty. Besides there s. iere is more behind all the clamour for an increased birth-rate. I have not forgotten what a certain Lane, shire medical officer had to say about the treat: ment of infants, After noting that the infantile mortality rate of 145 in his district showed a gradual decline, he added, "So many of these deaths are preventible that it is a standing dis_ grace to the community that the number is so great. The disgustingly-filthy so-called 'com- forter' should be abolished," he declared—as I ventured to do only the) other week. The in- ventor of that abomination of desolation has Much to answer for. IN THE COURTS.

"GIVE ME SIX MONTHS."

Prisoner Who Forecasted His Sentence

A prisoner, whose language, even in the dock, was more forcible than polite, appeared before the Mayor and other magistrates at the Police Court on Monday, in the person of John Har- greaves, of no fixed abode, who was charged with having been, drunk and disorderly and also with having used threats to Joseph Woods, the manager at the "Express" Office. When the charge was read out, prisoner be- came excited and said: "Wait till I get my money first. I am not going to be tried before I get the money. He owes me is. Hid.; and you can give me six months if I get that." P.C. Willis said prisoner was in the "Express" Office, making use of threatening language to the manager. He said he was not afraid to be locked up and threatened to " knife " Mr. Woods. Prisoner (to the Chief Constable): You are bound to say I threatened him. I want my money before I say anything. You can give me what you like afterwards! Mr. Woods said the ,man's language was abominable. He did not think Hargreaves was really right. Prisoner burst into vituperative rhetoric, re- marking to the witness: "I am right for you. You Bench, "I want is. 111 d. off that rogue. I will do more for him yet." _

--The Clerk: Have you anything to say? Prisoner: What about my money? The Clerk: Do you owe him any money, Mr. Woods?

Mr. Woods: N The money was 14,- on the counter and the officer came back itu• it. I think there was 111 d.

The Mayor: Does he sell papers for you? Mr. Woods: No. He has tried the same trick before. I do not think it is safe for him to be at large. The Chief Constable (to the magistrates) : You have heard a good deal of what he has to say about six months. It is twithin your province to give him that. He ought to find sureties to ensure his being of good behaviour. A good deal of this filthy, disgusting language is affected. He is trying to convey to the Court that he is a bit mad. He knows perfectly well what he is doing. He would eat the whole of us iii. Court if we listened to him. The Governor of Liverpool Prison said some time ago that he was not a fit subject for prison discipline, but my opinion is that he is fit for it and that it is what he wants for the rest of his life. The Mayor (to prisoner) : You will find two sureties of .tlO each to be of good behaviour, or go to prison for six Months. Prisoner: I Will do the six months!

BOROUGH POLICE COURT

TUESDAY.

Before Councillor H. Roberts (in the chair), Mr. C. Jackson Holmes and Mr. T. Locker.

_ Assault on a Wife.—Thomas Miley, gas stoker, 19, Longford-street, was charged with assault- ing his wife, Mary Miley, on Saturday.—ThO evidence showed that prisoner had been ill- using his wife for some considerable time. On Saturday morning he went home for some money, and when he returned at 2.30 in a drunken condition he assaulted his wife without any provocation.--Sergt. Metcalfe said Miley was very drunk when he was arrested. He re- fused to put his boots on to accompany witness to the Police Station, and it was eventually found necessary to take him into custody in a half-dressed condition. In the house and on the way to the Police Station prisoner used bad language.—Prisoner was ordered to pay an in- clusive penalty of 25., with the alternative of 14 days' imprisonment.

Lodging-house Property Recovered.—William Baxter, labourer, 17, Tanner's-lane, was charged with stealing on October 3rd, a cloth valued at 25., the property of Margaret McCabe, from a lodging-house, 9, Stanley-street. Bridget Coney alias Emily Baxter or Smith, of the same ad- dress, was charged with stealing a bed quilt and a bed sheet, valued at Bs. 6d., from the same lodging-house. Some of the articles were found to have been pawned, and later Detective-Ser- geant Reddington arrested the woman.—The prisoners were remanded for a week. WEDNESDAY.

Before Messrs. J. W. Hayward, C. Jackson

Holmes and W. T. Williams.

An Unprovoked Assault.—Michael Kelly (21), labourer, 71, Pierpoint-street, Warrington, was charged with assaulting Michael Wall, a farm labourer at Bewsey Old Hall.—The evidence showed that Wall was in the Imperial Hotel on Monday evening. Between seven and eight o'clock in the evening the prisoner entered the bar and struck the complainant a violent blow in the face, with the result that he was ren- dered partially unconscious.—Detective-Ser- geant neddington stated he had made inquiries and found that the assault had been committed without any provocation, and that the com- plainant had to be attended to at the Infirmary. —Prisoner was sent to gaol for a month with hard labour.

Army Sergeant Charged with Desertion.— Sergt. Thomas Seddon (27). of the 4th Battalion Lancashire Fusiliers, stationed at Barrow-in- Furness, was charged with being a deserter from his regiment.—The evidence showed that pris- oner had been sent to Wigan to take a deserter to the headquarters of the regiment. but he had himself partaken of too much drink.—Prisoner said he would reserve his statement.—The Chief Constable suggested that prisoner ghould be left in the hands of the police, who would hand him over to the military escort when it arrived. —The Bench agreed.

STOeKTON HEATH.

MORE LIGHT.

Petition from Hill Chile Trustees.

LAMPS TO BE ERECTED.

- At the fortnightly meeting of the Runcorn Rural District Council on Tuesday a petition was read from the trustees. of the Hill Cliffe Baptist Chapel asking that gas lamps should be fitted from Red-lane Bridge to the chapel. This, it was pointed out, would be of great conveni- ence to householders, and would be a safeguard against accidents. Councillor Mclntyre said that the lighting of the road would be a distinct charge on the parish of Appleton. There had been an acci- dent at Red-lane Bridge quite recently, and that was attributed to the fact that there were no lights on the Appleton side and that the driver of a motor car had been misguided by a light on the Whitefield-road side. He thought the petition might be granted. Councillor Britch : I am surprised at the "impertinence" of one of the members from Stockton Heath in attributing this accident to there not being any lights on the Appleton side of the bridge. It is the Stockton Heath people who are responsible for putting their lights in such awkward positions. Councillor Linaker : They are an awkward lot as a rule. _ .

Councillor Mclntyre: Councillor Britch is under a misconception of the matter. It was decided to grant the petition, and also that the arrangement of the lights be left in the hands of Councillors Brach, Pilling, Hallows and Mclntyr6.

===== 0002601_19141010_art0125.txt | included=true | included =====
DEATH OF MRS. BLACK.

Last of a Noted Scottish Family. It is with deep regret that we have to an- nounce the death of Mrs. Mary 'Ann Black, which occurred with tragic suddenness at the residence of her son, 52, Whitefield-road, on Sunday. Mrs. Black was 67 years of age, and the widow of Mr. William Black, superintend- ent of the Edinburgh and D4trict Water Trust. She comes of a very well-known family in the north of Scotland, her mother being a Rost- a relativd of Sir Charles Ross, of Balnagown, who was the inventor of the Ross rifle, which is now being used by the Canadian troops at the front. The Tomson family, of which Mrs. Black is about the last direct representative, can trace

their ancestry back to the 11th century. The circumstances of the death were related at the inquest, which was conducted at Stockton Heath Police Station on Tuesday by Mr. E. A. White. •

William Donald Black, journalist, was the first witness. He said that his mother, who re- sided with him, had an apoplectic fit two years ago, and specialists then told him that her heart was diseased. Her health had since been indifferent and about five or six months ago she was attended by Dr. Howard. Dr. Flannagan, of Manchester, had told him that she might drop down dead any minute. She retired to bed about 910 p.m. on Sunday, and a moment later he heard a thud. Hurrying to the bedroom he found his mother unconscious on the floor. She

was not breathing then. He was not surprised at her sudden death after what doctors had told him.

Dr. Howard deposed that death .was due to a clot of blood forming in the right ventricle of the heart and being carried to the lungs. The cause of death was a natural one. A verdict of "Death from natural causes" was returned.

Mr. T. S. Steel watched the proceedings on behalf of the relitives. The funeral took place on Thursday at the Newington Cemetery, Edinburgh, where the mortal remains of the deceased lady's husband and other relatives are interred.

THE BRIGADE.

The Comic Side of a Motor Car Fire.

COUNCILLOR BOTTOMLEY AND RIDICULE Councillor Mclntyre (the chairman) made a verbal report to the Parish Meeting on Monday night on the subject of the village fire brigade. The brigade, he said, were now "getting pretty well under way." They were nearing a settlement in regard to a place where they could have the fire bell fixed and where the apparatus could be stored.. They had forwarded a request to the District Council to abandon the box, on the square and take up permanent room near Mr. Baguley's smithy. The bell would be put up on a space provided at the Police Station. There had, the speaker continued, been several fires since the last meeting. The fires at Miss Partington's school and Mr. Knight's establish- ment at London Bridge, were dealt with in a satisfactory manner, the brigade doing good work. They had a call to Mr. Parton's laundry, where they found that that gentleman's motor car was on fire. This outbreak had its humor- ous side, for someone called up the Police Station on the telephone, and the Inspector said: "Do YOU want the brigade?" Those who rang up said "We want you to come and have a look at it." (Laughter.) So he went. (Renewed laughter.) Councillor Westbrook: To look at the brigade or the fire? (Laughter.) The Chairman: To look at the car on fire. It was about 11.15 and some young " bloods " com- ing off the car got the apparatus out and went to the fire, which was put out shortly afterwards. There were no firemen there, but several of the committee men were. Councillor Bottoraley : I was present at that fire, and I was there when the bell rang. I must give all credit to those persons who took the fire apparatus to the building. They certainly did it expeditiously. When you tell me there were no firemen there, I can only say that I am sorry.

Those who were there a.;(1 their best, but I was sorry to find there was no nozzle on the hose, and that they could not get the jet to carry more than a yard without it. Had a house been on fire instead of a motor car there would have been a lot of trouble. That oversight, however, is explained by the fact that there were no fire- men present. PARISH MEETING.

Adequate Maintenance for Soldiers and Dependents.

-COUNCILLOR ROSE'S OPINION.

The half-yearly meeting of the Parish Council, which was held in the Church Room on Monday evening, passed oft very quietly. Indeed, the ratepayers appeared to be well satisfied with the manner in which the affairs of the parish are conducted, and listened with interest to the brief resume of the past six months' work given by Councillor Mclntyre (chairman bf the Parish Council), who presided. • There wag, naturally, a "breeze" over Fire Brigade matters, which is reported fully in an- other column.

Mr. E. Bradshaw (clerk) and several council- lors were amongst those present.

PARISH WPRK SATISFACTORY. Before speakingipn the business of the Coun- cil, the Chairman explained that it had been

suggested that that meeting might possibly be abandoned this year on account of their being so engrossed in other matters. He, for one, was not agreeable to that without consulting the ratepayers of the parish, and so they were met there to discuss the affairs of the parish. The work of the Council, continued Councillor Mclntyre, had gone on very satisfactorily during the past six months. Matters of lighting had received special attention, and he felt sure they would all appreciate the improvement brought about by the extra lamps provided at Lumb Brook. The danger in that locality had now been removed.

He thought the street-making of the district was very satisfactory, and he was of the opinion that there was no other parish in a better posi- tion with regard to streets and passages. (Hear, hear.) Egerton-street and Brackley-street would be attended to before long, and, indeed, Egerton- street and Alexandra-street had already been scheduled for making. THE WAR.

===== 0002601_19141010_art0129.txt | included=true | included =====
Suggested Scale of Assistance.

Councillor Rose proposed the following reso- lution: "That this meeting calls upon the Gov- ernment to arrange at the earliest possible moment, a definite scheme of liberal assistance to wives and dependents of our men killed or maimed in the war." Continuing, Councillor Rose said that in the estimation of all civilised countries, the British Empire and its resources had gone ,up inestimably, and that was owing to the class of men they had at the front. It

had got to be a serious question in the country as -to what class and how many more men they would have at the front, unless something definite were done to ensure those men that their dependents would be looked after. He thought the resolution would commend itself to everyone. If they let the matter pass now, and nothing definite was done, the persons affected would come badly off when the was was over. Mr. G. Knight seconded. • Councillor Dukes said he did not know what effect the resolution was likely to have on the Government, but if the passing of it was likely to move them, he did not mind supporting it. However, he did not know which way it was going to be done. They were crying out for support now, when they had sacrificed the means whereby they could have obtained that support. Everyone was busy talking about what the men ought to do, now that they were gone. A great deal had to be done, but it should have been in hand at the commencement. If the people responsible for advising the men to go had taken such a question that had been raised into consideration at first they would have been further on the way to accomplishing something. He held the opinion that the defence of a nation was equal in merit to any other function per- formed by it, no matter what it might be. They had the ridiculous spectacle of men acting as amateur policemen at ss. a day, whilst the trained policemen were on the battlefield getting Is. 3d. per day and no assurance that their de- pendents would be looked after. He could not understand why, after eight weeks, they were only just talking of what they ought to do for the men who had enlisted. Even the Labour party were refusing to participate in any re- cruiting campaign unless they could be assured of more adequate treatment. Whatever ser- vices a nation required should be paid for more liberally than any other. The present charity' mongering called for the condemnation of every man. Before advising anyone to take on respon- sibilities which some of the advisers were not prepared to take on, they should see that such resolutions as the one proposed reached tlie right quarters, and then say for what they were prepared to barter their Jabour. Councillor Rose: What enlightened body has thought of taking a lead in the matter? I am only doing my little best to see that these mat- ters are being looked after. Councillor' Dukes: I have no desire to belittle the resolution, but we ask for adequate main- tenance. Some people , say 17s. is -adequate, whilst .an " M.P." says £7 is adequate. The Chairman: The 17s. is not correct. A wife with five children will get 225. to 245. Councillor Bottomley : I don't think any man who enlists as a soldier or his dependents should be dependent upon charity. They have a right by law to have a reasonable allowance made to them. (Hear. hear.) Councillor Rose: We want a definite scheme I of liberal assistance from the Government. I I don't hold that if a man's life is given, a "quid" is at all adequate. It ought to be " quid pro quo." It was decided to send the resolution, which was carried unanimously, to the Prime Minis- ter, the Member for the Knutsforcl Division, the Leader of the Unionist party, and the Liberal and Labour parties, Sze.

LYM M.

Special Reserve.—About 20 members of the Special Reserve were called from Lymm on Thursday.

Over £5OO for Relief Fund.—Lymm has con- tributed in no niggardly fashion to the National Relief Fund, over £5OO having already been forwarded from the district.

Local Wedding.—At the Bowdon Wesley Church on Monday afternoon Miss Annie Guest was married to Mr. John Elliot. Both Parties have resided in Lymm for some years and the bride has been an active member of the Wes- ley-an .Church Choir.

Football.—The Lymm Wednesday P.C. Visited PendleburY on Wednesday and suffered defeat by 41-2. The committee are attempting to arrange a match with Stockport for Wednesday next. Mr. Blount has resigned the secretary- ship, which has been undertaken by Mr. Reed, of The Grove, Lymm. United Free Church Services.—The first of a series of united services, under the auspices of the Free Church Council, was held in the Con- gregational Church on Wednesday evening. In the unavoidable absence of the Pastor Mr. J. D. B. Smith conducted the service. There was a good attendance. The next service will be held on the first Wednesday in November, in the Wesleyan Methodist Church.

Local Loyalists in Good Health.—lt is grati- fying to learn that the young men who have left Lyinm for military training are all enjoy- ing good health, and from reports forwarded home from time to time seem as comfortable as can be expected under the circumstances. The churches and Sunday schools have lost several earnest workers, who have felt it their duty to take up arms for their King and country.

Council Elections.--Nominations for the vacancy caused by the resignation of the Rector from the • District Council closed . yesterday. There are two prospective candidates. They are Mr. Sam Bidder, contractor, Brook-lane, Lymm, who is proposed by Mr. W. E. Hillier and seconded by Mr. H. S. Saville, jun., and Mr. Alexander Mclntyre, farmer, Burford-lane, who is proposed and seconded by Messrs. J. T. Clarke and H. Gotobed respectively. H arvest F est iva s.---Speeial harvest festival services were held at the Wesleyan Church on Sunday, when the Rev. Dixon, of the Warring- ton Circuit, preached to good congregations, morning and evening. Similar services were held the same day at the Broom Edge Primitive Methodist Chapel, the Rev. W. Daw, of Altrinc- ham, preaching in the ,afternoon and evening, whilst on Monday the Lymni Primitive Metho- dist Choir rendered the service of song "Royal Oak." The in-gathering of the harvest was also celebrated at the Bucklow Hill Congrega- tional Church, the special preachers being Mx. Samuel Lamb, of Bowdon; in the morning, and Mr. G. B. Wilson, of Hale, in the evening. The collections for the day totalled just over £3O.

===== 0002601_19141010_art0132.txt | included=true | included =====
"SAFELY GATIIERED IN."

Harvest Thanksgiving at the Parish

Church.

The thanksgiving services for the safe in- gathering of the harvest were held at Lymm Parish Church on Sunday. In the morning the Rev. L. H. Tiarks, the Vicar of Thelwall, was the special preacher, and in the evening, the Rev. F. 0. Pcole, Rector of Nantwich, occupied the pulpit. .At each service the choir rendered special music, Mr. William Cook presiding at the organ. _ _ _ _ _

The church had been beautifully decorated. A large red cross stood out in bold relief against the other adornments of the pulpit, whilst all the window ledges had been tastefully treated. The decorations in the chancel and around the font were not the least effective. Preaching at the evening service, from the words "Our Father" (St. Luke xi. 2), the Rev. F. 0. Poole drew the attention of the congre- gation to the wealth of meaning embraced in the syllables of the text. For instance, they set one thinking of the earthly father who was always willing to answer the questions of his little children, willing to solve all their diffi- culties, to comfort them in their troubles, and to provide them with all the necessities of life. When Christ came on the earth He showed that God was ever ready to comfort, to guide, and to provide all the things that Were necessary for bodily and spiritual sustenance. THE SUPREME TEACHING OF CHRIST.

..... Had the congregation ever thought that that was the supreme teaching of Jesus Christ:— that everything whiCh He suffered and taught was directed to leading them to the one thing, viz., that they might be restored to the position Which they had lost possession of as the sons of God? Christ came to rescue the world from sin and enable them to live as children of God. It seemed to him that when they celebrated their harvest festivals they were reminded of this fact in no uncertain way. As they looked round they saw .on all hands symbols that reminded them that God was their Father. Why were they met in church that night? It was in order to thank God—the God they had been worshOping and praising during the year—that He had once again thought good to give them the things necessary for the body. How thankful they ought to be this year, especially considering the terrible times through which the nation was passing. When the war began there was almost a panic. They thought that their country would run short of food and they began to look forward to terrible times— to times when the poor would cry out for bread which could not be provided, and when prices would nisi' up to such a height .that few only would be able to get sufficient for their needs. CAUSE FOR SPECIAL GRATITUDE. How grateful they ought to be to God, there- fore, for giving them a bountiful harvest and fine weather to gather it in, and also for His Providence in keeping open the trade routes, which allowed ,an incessant stream of food to come into the country. Although the harvest festival served to remind them of the Father- hood of God, he was afraid they often forgot that very important fact so far as their daily lives were concerned.

AN EXTRAORDINARY CHANGE. They had been complaining during the last few years that people seemed to be giving them- selves up too much to pleasure and amusement, that the people's lives were becoming so full of other things that they had no time or 'energy to devote to the spiritual life, which, in colN- quence, seemed to be getting crushed out of the nation. Yet what an extraordinary change had been witnessed during the last few weeks. It needed something from Almighty God to re- mind them of His presence and that He was their Father. __ A NATION IN PRAYER. _

Very soon after the outbreak of war, a day was set aside for special, intercession, a day which was kept throughout the Church. A newspaper described that day of intercession as " a nation on its knees "—not in craven ,fear, but on'its knees remindful that God was their Father and laying its trouble at His feet. Was there not a danger that When all the trouble was over they might sink, back again into the morass of indifference? Would they again for- get the Fatherhood of God? It would be well for them to use the harvest festivals as a re- minder each Year that God was th'eir Father! The following were responsible for the decora- tions: Font, Mrs. Johnson; pulpit, Mrs. New- march and Mrs. Beckett; chancel, Mrs. and the Misses Callender ;. screen, Miss Hill, Miss Wat- kin and Mrs. Bttrrows; windows, Mrs. T. W. F. Dixon, Mrs. Boumphrey, Mrs. Giles Hunt, Mrs. Evans, Mrs. Scragg and Miss Goffin, Mrs. Cook and Af4.s. Ditchfield, Miss Evans, Mrs. and Miss Saville, Miss Moulsworth, Mrs. and Miss Stewart Sinith, Miss Garton and Mrs. Palmer.

For the Hospital.

The fruit and the flowers used in the decora- tions were sent to No. 2 Westerrf General Hos- pital, Manchester.

ONE EFFECT OF THE WAR. Proposed Sewerage Scheme Held Up.

At a meeting of the Runcorn District Coun- cil on Tuesday, the Grappenhall Sewerage Com- mittee recommended (1) that in view of the fact that tenders for the Grappenhall sewerage scheme are 50 per cent, in excess of the engi- neer's estimates, laid before the Local Govern- ment Board Inspector at the inquiry, and that the rate of interest on bound capital has also gone up half per cent., the carrying out of the scheme be postponed, and that these facts be laid before the Local Government Board with a view to a reconsideration of the whole scheme; (2) that the Clerk write to Messrs. R. Davies and Co., explaining the committee's reason for postponing the carrying out of the scheme and asking whether under all the circumstances Admiral Jones-Parry will renew the option granted the Council for the purchase of the out- fall site for a further period of 12 months. Councillor G. Pilling moved the adoption of the report and said the real cause of the pro- posed postponement was increased cost of materials.

Councillor Lister-Kaye seconded and said it was the thing he had stuck out for for many months. The scheme would probably mean about 2s. 6d. in the X on the rates before it was finished, and the committee thought that it should be deferred until times were better. Ile also moved that there be added to the report the fact that the local committee would meet and see if they could not suggest some tempo- rary remedy for the existing grievance. Councillor Linaker : Is the postponement sug- gested because of the extra cost of materials? Councillor Pilling replied to the effect that that was one of the causes. The Chairman (Councillor C. E. Maker, sen.): We felt that this was the best thing to do. The report was adopted.

LATCHFORD WITHOUT.

Refuse Removal—The Runcorn Rural District Council on Tuesday confirmed the action of the Latchford Without Parish Council in arrang- ing for the removal of refuse in the district.— Councillor Pilling raised the matter, and it was pointed out that the cost would be a charge on the parish.

===== 0002601_19141010_art0138.txt | included=true | included =====
MOORE .

Miss Banner Married.—A pretty wedding was solemnised at the Wesleyan Church on Wed- nesday, when Miss Susan Banner, who is well- known in connection with the Sunday School, was married to Mr. George Davidson, of Rugby. The officiating cleric was the Rev. H. T. Dixon, of Penketh. The bride wore a navy blue cos- tume, with heliotrope-coloured hat, and the bridesmaids, the Misses Bella and Bessie Davidson, were attired in Saxe blue costumes, with black velvet hats, trimmed with roses.— On Sunday Miss Banner was the recipient of parcel of household linen from the teachers, choir and members of the church. Miss Dutton made the presentation and Miss Banner suitably responded.

Exciting Football Match.—On Saturday the Walton club visited the Daresbury ground, where a fairly evenly contested game was played. In the first half the Daresbury men had the best of the game and managed to get two goals to their credit, the scorers being W. M. Cartwright and J. Kippax. In the second half Cartwright broke through the home de- fence and scored the visitors' first goal. The game was continued at a fast pace and it was not until five minutes before time that IT. Marsh equalised for the visitors.

===== 0002601_19141010_art0139.txt | included=true | included =====
PAD GATE!

Wesley Gu i Id.—" Parables of Prayer" was the subject taken by the Rev. A. T. Morton at the monthly devotional meeting of the Wesley Guild on Monday evening. The speaker men- Honed that there were many evidences that the present crisis had been the means of drawing many. people back to the churches.—Miss E. Bennett read a communication from headquar- ters, illustrating the spirit which ought to obtain in the Guild during the war; and Miss Alice Brough gave a sweet rendition of "In the Secret of His Presence."

Church Lads' Brigade.—This movement is making very rapid strides in the Padgate dis- trict, for, although it is only nine weeks since the Brigade was first started, under the able leadership of Mr. Dickinson, the membership is now practically one hundred. There have been several previous attempts to form a bri- gade in the district, but in each case the effort has failed. A few weeks ago the boys were camped out in the Vicarage grounds, and on Sunday they joined in a route march.

===== 0002601_19141010_art0140.txt | included=true | included =====
PENIKETH.

Wounded Warrior's Prize.—The general meeting and prize distribution of the Penketh, Sankey and District Garden and Cage Bird Society took place on Friday of last week Oa the Wesleyan Schoolroom. It was reported that the show funds this year were materially affected by labour troubles, With the result that there was a deficit. Members and Com- petitors, however, agreed to make food as soon as possible. Thelshow itself had been a highly satisfactory one. When the prizes were being distributed by the Chairman (Mr. W. Woods) the name of Mr. J. Dampson was called out- as that of the winner of the cup offered in the Cage Bird Section by Captain Evans. It was stated that the winner had gone to the front before the show took place and that since then he had been reported as wounded. '

Entangled in a Threshing Machine.—Hugh Pownall, of Station-road, Penketh, , a well- known resident, while cleaning up a threshing outfit, when the day's work was over, on Thurs- day, acOdentally got his leg entangled in the drum 6f the machine, receiving such injury to the limb that amputation, it is feared, .will be found necessary.

GREAT SANKEY.

War Fund Concert.—A very successful con- cert in aid of the local War Fund was held in St. Mary's Schoolroom on Thursday, evening, the following artistes taking part: Miss Bessie Brough (soprano), Madame Jennie Armstrong (contralto), Mr. J. E. Hannan (tenor), Mr. E. Bate (bass), and ]Nlr. A. Dutton (elocutionist), Mr. E. Hannah presiding at the piano. During 'a brief interval in the programme 31r. C. E. Parker reminded the- audience that Hall Nook was being opened as a temporary home for desti- tuteßelgian refugees, and asked local residents to help in the matter by contributing either furniture, clothes, or money. The cost of 30 Persons for six months, he said, was calculated at _t3so.—The Vicar (the Rev. J. R. Jones) still- ported the appeal .—The concert realised £5.

Play at this juncture was of an even character, chances on both sides going a-begging in a manner which made one long for a Fish, who, probably, would have turned several openings to good account. Jolly, here- abouts, was playing an acceptable game, and was equal to all the calls made upon him, but he has even yet to reach the level of his last year's form. He had more work to do than his vis-a-vis, but Launce was undoubtedly playing a splendid game. When half-time arrived neither side held an advantage, the score sheet being of virgin whiteness.

===== 0002601_19141010_art0145.txt | included=true | included =====
SALFORD RE-START WELL.

Immediately after the resumption of the game the Salford men assumed the aggressive, and Baker was tackled whilst in possession. The home threequarters, benefiting from a scrum, rushed into the visitors' territory, but Launce averted danger by making a mark. The relief accruing was only of a temporary nature, for Warrington were soon attacking once more. John was responsible for getting his side out of a tight corner, but Tranter, catching the ball, punted high. Launce secured, but Tranter, again dashing the hopes of the visitors, followed up and grassed the runaway before he could get into his stride. Taberner made a rare dash for the line and crossed over near the flag, but the point was disallowed on the ground that he' had previously stepped into touch. For some, min- utes the game was waged in mid-field, where , Brookes, picking up, passed nicely to Fearnley, who, however, threw away wildly, a good oppor- tunity being thus lost. Young brought off a grand save when hard pressed by the opposing forwards, and after a series of scrummages on the home line, Dainteth cleared. Bevan troubled Jolley, who had to kick into touch to save the situation. At this stage Salford were making desperate efforts to score and succeeded in keeping the play in the Warrington half of the field for a considerable period, as periods in a football match .go. A scrummage almost in the mouth of the Warrington goal looked ominous, but rescue was at hand, and the ball was sent downfield. Baker, returning a kick- back by Launce, fielded brilliantly and made a good run, but he was grounded before he got into a really dangerous position.

A SPLENDID OPENING GOES A-BEGGING., The ball came into the hands of the War- rington threequarters and it was again punched up-field. Launce caught it, but his attempt at finding touch was unsuccessful, Tranter secur- ing and gaining ground for his side. Dainteth met with a slight injury, which necessitated a brief respite in hostilities. A free kick fell to , the lot of Warrington for obstruction, but Jolley, who took it, sent wide. The visitors found. the Warrington forwards "a tough nut to crack," and in order to cope with their work in the open resorted to long kicking. Mesley made a mark when just in the Warrington half, but his kick was a failure. Jolley gath- ered the ball, and after a neat little run, fol- lowed up by a good punch, put the home team once more on the attack. John, however, brought off a fine save when hard pressed by Critchley. . Taberner spoiled a splendid open- ing by knocking on a pass, when he had only got Launce to overcome. It was at this juncture . that Thomas found that his injury necessitated his leaving the field. It was evident that the visitors found the Warrington. tackling very , effective, and as they found that they could not break through, they resorted to making marks when other courses were open. Following upon a mark by Launce, Mesley tapped the ball past the home forwards, but the tactic proved in- effective, the leather being speedily despatched over the touch line. A THRILLING FINISH.

'The visitors indulged in a desperate rush on the Warrington citadel, but their incursion 11 proved to be only "a flash in the pan," for they Were soon hemmed in their own quarters. 11 Tranter secured from a scrummage just over the 11 half-way line, and before the Salfordians had " hardly realised what was happening dropped a beautiful goal. This, the first score of the after- noon, was warmly received by the crowd and it had the effect of putting the visitors on their mettle. Infusing plenty of dash into their play they raided the home territory, and on one occa- sion looked like bringing off the good thing. But the home back proved quite equal to the I occasion, dealing with the situation in a manner which met with the hearty approval of the on- lookers. The attackers soon became defeuders!' play being smartly transferred to the other end of the field. There the Warrington van worked the ball over the line and Young completed the try, amid the loud plaudits of the crowd. Jolley took the kick, but was unable to add the minor points. Subsequently play was of a vigorous character, and, nearing the end, Loveluck and Callender had both to receive the attention of the ambulance men. A minute or so afterwards Launce was slightly injured in attempting to save from Dainteth. The visitors forced the pace in an endeavour to minimise their deficit account, but all their efforts were unavailing. Green effectively checked a forward movement by the visiting sextet, making a beautiful mark, which Jolley endeavoured to turn to account, but without success, the ball falling short. When the whistle blew the score-board announced the result as follows Warrington, 1 goal 1 try (5 points); Salford, nil.

===== 0002601_19141010_art0146.txt | included=true | included =====
WARRINGTON'S WIN ,DUE TO THEIR FOR

Warrington owed their victory in the main to the brilliant work put in by their forwards. The club has long been noted for possessing a sterling front rank, and the present half-dozen who compose it are worthily maintaining tra- ditions. On Saturday they were full of enter- prise, and their combination left little room for criticism. Their cross-dribbling harassed the Salford threequarters, especially in the second half, when the pack were seen at their best. Mather, who first came under notice when play- ing for one of the local junior clubs, and then, was given a chance With the Warrington second string, fully justified his inclusion in the first team, proving a valuable man in the front rank, both for weight and ability. Dribbling is a more important asset than many Northern Union teams are apt to think, and Warrington proved the fact to Salford be- yond all reasonable doubt. Jolley again shone at full-back, especially in the second moiety, when he made the best pos- sible use of his gift of' speed. All the threequarters worked worthily, but if a distinction must be drawn, premier distinction falls to Tranter, whose huge and well-judged kicks were always of service to hjs side. Brookes, too, was a most useful man, rarely failing in his .task of breaking up the visitors' attacks. And then there was Baker; who showed himself a very handy man in tight corners, and, perhaps, played his best game this season. Taberner was a bit slow in waking up, but when he (lid he gave a good account of himself. Young was the best of the half-backs, but Dainteth gave him loyal support. Launce played a safe game for Salford, and Thomas and Lovelock worked well on the three- quarter line. Waite proved a handy man in the half-back line, and the whole of the forwards worked hard, but when it came to a matter of loose tactics they were no match for Warring- ton. „...

TO-DAY'S TEAM.

Warrington will be represented against Runcorn to-day, at Runcorn, by the following: Jolley; Baker, Brookes, Tranter, and Taberner ; Young and Dainteth; Cox, Clare, Fearnley, Green, Mather, and Critchley.

===== 0002601_19141010_art0149.txt | included=true | included =====
WARRINGTON "A " v. SALFORD.

The Warrington Combination, team made a journey to Salford on Saturday, in order to oppose the second string of , the home club. Early on in the game the visitors were aggres- sors, and their pertinacity was rewarded when they opened the scoring with a good try, ob- tained per Cunliffe. The home team were soon avenged, May equalising the scores and soon after Wards giving his side the lead by means of a beautiful try, to which Hampson added the goal points. Bevan further increased the homesters' lead by a dropped goal, Harri- son following with another a few minutes later. When the final whistle sounded the score stood: Salford, 3 goals 2 tries (12 points); Warrington, 1 try (3 points). '

ANGLING.

Warrington Working Men's Association.

N.A. CENTRE.

THE WAR FUND MATCH. The above cutest took place at London Bridge length on Saturday, both sides of the bridge being occupied. In view of the short notice the committee had to arrange the contest and search for prizes, the entry was very satisfactory, 263 competitors taking part. The aficials provided thirty prizes, and the match on the whole was a great success. The winner weighed in lib. 9oz. 14dr., and the lowest weight in the prizes was Boz. Bdr. Competition was keen between R. Clark, of Platt Bridge, and H. Moores, of Warrington; neither knew who had got through until the weigher came along. Clarke had 23 fish and Moore 29, but when the fish were put on the scales Clarke was found to be the winner by over 4oz. The contest was a great success. It is thought that the amount of money to be handed over to the War Fund will be something like .615 or .616. If contests could only be arranged by other Associations in the district, the War Fund would greatly benefit. We take the liberty of thanking all the prize donors for their kindness, and we can only feel we have done some little service to those our soldiers have left behind. Warringtonians ought to be pleased to learn that they are the first in the angling world to come forward with a scheme of this description.

RIOT NEAR CALCUTTA:

The Bengal Government announce that the Sikh passengers who were refused permission to land at Vancouver and who were sent home from, Canada refused to use the train provided to take them back to the Punjab and eventually began a hot fire on the police with revolvers, killing five persons. Troops shot 16 of the rioters, and others were arrested, the number accounted for being nearly 120.

===== 0002601_19141010_art0158.txt | included=true | included =====
Elmwood Congregationalists Get a Shook.

===== 0002601_19141010_art0159.txt | included=true | included =====
BANK QUAY RECS. MAKE II GREAT EFFORT.

On Saturday 'the average number of games in the local League was played and contested under very __favourable _conditions.

The Y.ll-I.C.A. received the Baptists as visi- tors and gained the victory by four goals against nov. The score is scarcely a true reflex of the game, as the visitors had their share. of the play, and threw many chances of scoring away by their forwards' erratic shoot- ing. The home forwards, on the other hand, netted the ball on four occasions. The visitors made several changes in their team from the previous week, which failed to come up to .their committee's expectations.

. On the Lovely-lane ground a very keen game was contested between St. Barnabas' and White Star teams, there being very little difference in the play, but in the end St. Barnabas' were victors by two goals against one. The Paddington team paid a visit to Newton and were entertained by the Cons. A fairly good game ensued, and in the first half the scores stood one goal each. The visitors fell away during the seco.ml portion and had to retire defeated by five goals against two. - A very keen struggle was witnessed at Pad- gate-lane between Fairfield 'United and Bank Quay Rees. As the game progressed all the old-time rivalry and keenness was shown, and at the interval the homesters were only leading by one goal to nil. In the SOC ond portion the Recs, appeared to play with fresh life and vigorously attacked the home goal, but they failed to break down the defence, . while the Unit'ed forwards managed to increase their 'lead and won the match bY two clear goals. The Earlestown Mission team visited Elm- wood Congregational, where they gained their , first victory in a very decisive manner, win- ning by five.goals against none. The home team: disappointed their supporters by their display. Longford United just managed to gain two points at the expense of Fairfield, Ent they were a little hit fortunato in this, as the visitors had only ten players during the whole game.

goal, which had a lucky escape in the first minute. The home team now got a penalty kick, from which they opened their score. They continued to press and obtained several corner-kicks, but failed to improve upon them. The visitors, aided by good individual efforts by their forwards, occasionally got near the home goal, but they did not receive much sup- port from their half-backs; and from one of these breakaways it was only smart goalkeep- ing by Potter that prevented a score being made. Half-time: United, one goal; Recs., nil. . .

, After changing ends the visitors had the wind in their favour, and quickly assumed the aggressive. Potter, in the home goal, was found plenty of work, and he was well sup- ported by Millward and Woodhouse. The game was strenuously contested, and the spec- tators were kept in a high state of excitement. One .of the visitors being fouled just outside the penalty area placed the home goal in dan- ger, the free-kick just passing wide of the, up- right. Following this relief the home forwards transferred play to the visitors' end, and, fol- lowing good {defensive play by Bushill, Berry and Smith, the home tcanx increased their lead. After this reverse the visitors played in desperate style and the home goalkeeper and 'backs were severely tested, the visitors gaining many corner-kicks, but failing to break down the home defence. At the call of time the result was :—Fairfield United, two goals; Bank Quay Recs., nil.

NEWTON CONS. v. PADDINGTON UNITED. The above teams met on the ground of the former club, and were represented by the fol- lowing players:— _

Newton Cons.: Dagnall; Tithers and Law- ranee; Lennox, Clark and Aiken; Beresford, Dodd, Rubotham, Smart and Weave.

Paddington: Knight; Holmes and Smith; Tay- lor, Garnett and Spencer; Pritchard, Bleasdale, Darbyshire, Wrench and Craik. , Referee, J. T. Hadkiss. The visitors won the toss Mid the home team kicked off against the wind. The opening ex- changes were very evenly contested, and follow- ing midfield play each end was visited in turn in quick succession, and the visitors were un- fortunate in having a goal disallowed for off- side. Following this escape the home forwards raced away and Lennox opened the score. Just before the interval the visitors equalised from a penalty kick. The homesters at once gained the upper hand in the second half, and Beresford scored twice in quick succession. Nettled by these reverses the visitors rallied and put the home team on the defence, and attacked strongly, when one of the home backs in attempting to clear kicked through his own goal. Rubotham and Smart scored again for the home team. Result: New- ton Cons., 5 goals; Paddington, 2 goals.

ELMWOOR.C9NGREGATIONAL v. EARLES-

TOWN ST. JOHN'S MISSION. The above teams met on the Cans.' ground at Manchester-road, the following players turning out:—

Elmwood: Nork ; Jos. Plicenix and Hazeldine; Pritchard, Jas. Phcenix and Dutton; Ellam, Gleave, Ashton, Wells and Lamb. _

Mission: Massey; Griffith and Winders; Theo- bald, F. Eaton and Taylor; Plumpton, Wil- liams, A. Eaton, Almond and Wallas. Referee, G. Bamber.

The game opened very evenly, and after fifteen minutes' play the visitors made an attack, when Williams hit the cross-bar and A. Eaton scored from the rebound. Encouraged by this success, the visitors had the best of the game, but were gradually driven back to mid-field, alid shortly before the interval Eaton scored again for the visitors, the home defence being much at fault. Half-time score: Mission, 2 goals; Cons., nil. The homesters were expected to show to better advantage with the wind behind them in the second half, but their efforts were not sus- tained, and gradually the Mission lads drove them back towards their own goal, where Hazel- dine unfortunately put the ball tiirough his own goal. This reverse appeared to have some effect on the home players, and their play suffered somewhat. The visitors were cOnstantly press- ing and further goals were scored by Williams and Wallas. Result: Earlestown Mission, 5 goals; Elmwood Cons., nil.

LONGFORD UNITED v. FAIRFIELD A.F.C. The above teams met on Saturday on the ground behind the Horse and Jockey Inn, Win- ' wick-road. The home team, kicked off against a strong wind. The opening movements were in favour of the visitors, and they were driven back by the home defenders. For a while the I lay was of the give and take order, after which I -3 'United forwards showed a great improve- r• •nt. in their game. From a centre by Cox from the extreme left, Finch opened the home team's account with a hard rising shot, War- burton having no chance. The visitors, who were playing with a fairly strong wind and only ten men, seemed to revive a little after this, but could not beat Brown in goal, although Roberts and Challinor tried him with decent shots. Bates was centring well, and the visit- ing forwards played good football, frequently ' combining well, only to have their efforts frus- trated by Mason and Holden. Half-time arrived with Longford leading by one goal to nil. On resuming the home team began well and pressed for a time, Cox shooting wide from very 11 short range. Finch and Smart were also very troublesome on the other wing, but Warburton saved well at the expense of one or two corners. Johnson and Jackson also did much to upset their efforts. Fairfield got- going and raided Longford goal area continually, testing Brown once or twice. However, they could not score, and Longford finished winners by one goal to nil.

WARRINGTON_ AND DISTRICT

LEAGUE.

EARLESTOWN Y.M.C.A. v. LATCHFORD BAPT I STS.

These teams opposed each other on the ground of the former, in the following order : Y.M.C.A. : E. Martin; Atkinson and Pope; Fairhurst, Goulden and H. Martin; Taylor, Hughes, Riley, Gilman and Lee. Baptists: Sturgess ; Shelmardine and E. Hardy; G. Hardy, Johnson and Albinson ; Haslam, Rodgers, Pearson, Stubbs and Blease. Referee, Mr. S. C. Essenhigh.

-- There was a good crowd to witness the game. The home captain won the toss. Pearson kicked off and both teams settled down to a good style of play. The home team were the first to become dangerous and a very easy chance of snoring was allowed to pass un- accepted. The visitors gained relief by kicking into touch; then they rushed the ball into the home quarters, where Martin had to save sev- eral warm shots, and a foul against the Bap- tists gave relief to the home defence. Fol- lowing mid-field play, Riley drew first blood for the Y.M.C.A. From the centre-kick the visitors did most of the attacking and missed several chances of equalising by bad shooting, and the good display of Martin in the home goal. At the opposite end Fairhurst just skimmed the bar. At half-time the Y.M.C.A. were leading by one goal to nil. 'On re-starting, the visitors were soon forced on the defensive and Sturr,ess saved at the expense of a corner. The 'sfiag-kick was well placed, and owing to slackness by the visitors' backs Hughes scored the second goal for the Y.M.C.A. Both teams now played vigorously. The homesters displayed the better combina- tion and were pulled up several times for in- fringements of the offside rule. These free- kicks enabled the visitors to pay a visit to the home end, which was of short duration, as they threw chances away by erratic shooting. Fairhurst scored the third goal for the home team. Following these reverses the visitors struggled hard, and one of their forwards failed to take the opportunity when he had an open goal to shoot at. Martin was severely tested, but he cleared his lines and set his for- wards going with a big punt. After several free-kicks for offside Sturgess conceded a fruitless corner. The game was now evenly contested for a while, and a few minutes before time Hughes registered the fourth goal. Re- sult: Y.M.C.A., four goals; Baptists, nil.

ST. BARNABAS', v. ORFORD WHITE STAR. These teams were opposed to each other on HO Lovely-lane ground, with the following players:— St. Barnabas': Shaw; E. Glover and Long; B. Glover, O'Neil and Foster; S. Smith, Walker, Taylor, Vernon and Houghton. White Star: Miller; Pilling and Harrison; Gifford, Denny and Caldwell; Readon, Dun- bobbin, Meachin, Hankinson and Allen. Referee, Mr. A. Holt. There was a fair number of spectators when the home captain won the toss and decided to play with the wind. Meachin set the ball rolling and the visitors quickly made a raid into the home territory, which was repulsed with a fine clearance by Long. Then the game was contested at a good pace and each end was visited, when a good effort for the home team was spoiled by offside tactics. The free-kick put the visitors into a good position and Shaw brought off a grand save, from which the home forwards raced away and Denny Vernon securing the leather shot straight for goal, where J. Denny, in attempt- ing to clear, turned the ball between the posts, Miller having no chance to save. The Stars made deSperate efforts to equalise, but met with a stout opposition, and at half-time the score was :—St. Barnabas', one goal; White Star, nil. On resuming, the game was keenly contested and the ball' travelled quickly from end to end. The defenders on both sides were play- ing a grand game, there being very little to Choose between the teams at this stage.. Grad- ually the visitors gained a little advantage, and following a brief attack on the home goal Gifford equalised the scores. Being on a level footing, both teams playedNmore energetically to obtain the lead, and the home forwards Proved to be more dangerous, but Pilling and Harrison defended in fine style, and O'Neil tried a long rising shot, which was going wide of the post. From a misunderstanding be- tween the visitors' defence the ball was partly cleared, when Smith raced in and gained the lead for the home team. The visitors now tried hard to get on level terms again, and the home goal had a lucky escape, as a well- intended shot struck one of the players in the back. No further scoring was done and the result was: St. Barnabas', two goals; Orford White Star, one goal.

R, SATURDAY, OCTOBER 10, 1914.

THE

Summary of Authentic News From All

Involved Areas.

===== 0002601_19141010_art0178.txt | included=true | included =====
SPECIALLY COMPILED FOR "THE EXAMINER."

TENTII

WEEK.

ANTWERP BESIEGED.

The siege of Antwerp is being pressed with vigour. Last Friday the German attack was still too far from the city for the sound of firing to be audible in the streets. The Germans had failed to break through the Belgian defence and their artillery fire was remarkably inaccurate. Saturday's report was less re-assuring. Under great pressure the Belgians had been compelled to withdraw from part of their outer line and take position behind the River Nethe, some ten miles from the city. In the abandoned ground stand several forts and redoubts. The Belgian Legation in London received the following message from Antwerp on Saturday : "On the east of the Senne the defence of Ant- werp has been forced to fall back to the Nethe before the violent attack of the German artil- lery, and after a desperate resistance of five days. The position on the Nethe is very strong. The Belgian Army will resist with its full energy." Refugees left the city in a constant stream this week, and many are crossing by steamer to England. On Tuesday the Belgian forces defending Ant- werp were firmly established on the line of the Rupel and the Nethe, against which the Ger- man attacks had failed. The enemy "pushed forwaro their position against considerable resistance by the garrison," and the Military Governor of Antwerp publicly informed the people that bombardment was im- minent. The Belgian Government transferred itself to Ostend.

===== 0002601_19141010_art0179.txt | included=true | included =====
GRAVE DANGER.

Antwerp was reported on Thursday to be in grave danger, the Germans having established themselves over the rivers Nethe and Scheldt, and become able to attack with effect the second fortified line. On Wednesday the enemy began to bombard the city. The besiegers' shells fell in the middle of the city. Several Zeppelins dropped bombs on the houses. The German force has been estimated at 125,000 men; the Belgians within the fortress should have an army at least half as strong. The civil population fled towards the Dutch frontier, but the King and Queen gallantly re- mained at their post.

===== 0002601_19141010_art0180.txt | included=true | included =====
THE BATTLE OF THE AISNE. TIIE LONGEST ENGAGEMENT IN HISTORY.

What is for the want of a better name called by the Battle of the Aisne continued through this week, and reaches its 29th day to-day. For many days the struggle, which extended right across the north-east of France, and really only touched the Aisne at the point where the British Expeditionary Force are sta- tioned, resolved itself into an extension of the French left wing towards the Belgian frontier and a lengthening of the German right in the same region to meet the danger of being out- flanked. It has been found that generals can- not, nowadays, provide surprise movements. Aviators on each side see to that. The French attempt to outflank the Germans has been de- tected, and the German device to meet the move by withdrawing men from the east has been discovered by the Allies. We even know that the transfer has taken place by means of

the railways via Namur. The Germans made a determined effort to stem the advance of the Allies in the district between Peronne and Novon, where they massed a large number of men. In Berlin it was claimed that this effort had been crowned with some success and that certain positions near Roye have been recovered. The continued advance of the Allies placed General voqi Kluck's army, the German right, in an increasingly dangerous position. To check this movement the enemy aimed a counter-attack at the French near where their line west of the Oise turns sharp eastward to follow the Aisne valley towards Champagne. At Roye, in this region, the German attempt was particularly violent. Paris was able to continue to report that the German attacks hid been. uniformly repulsed and wild rumours of complete victory in the north were circulated in Paris. Victory, as the Times correspondent at Bordeaux said, was in the air, but it was not yet a substantial fact. There was every reason for cheerfulness and good hope as to the final result. Thit the end was not yet. The battle, an official communication issued in Paris on Sunday night stated, was in full pro- gress in the Arras region. "The Allies have made progress in the Sois- sons region, where the enemy's trenches have been taken." As a set-off against the Allies' success at Soissons it was reported on Monday that north of the Oise fighting continued with great vio- lence, and that the Allied troops, who were re- ported as having resumed the offensive at several places in this region, had had "to yield ground at certain points." SUCCESSES ON THE EAST.

On the upper Meuse the Germans failed to cross near St. Mihiel, their temporary bridge being destroyed by the French. Further east in the Woevre the French were able to record fresh progress between St. Mihiel and Apremont, in the hills a few miles to the south-east. In the Argonne the 16th German Corps (of the Crown Prince's Army), which had tried to slip through the wood of La Grurie, was thrown back to the north of the route Varennes-La Harazee-Vienne-La-Ville. An announcement by the German General Staff at Berlin recorded this attempt in the ambiguous form of a remark that the German troops in the Argonne had "obtained substan- tial advantages in a southerly direction."

NEV GERMAN MOVE. Fresh Forces From Belgium to Aid Right Wing. ALLIES OVER GERMAN BORDER. On Tuesday Paris reported officially that the Allies' left wing was extending more and more towards the North; and that very large masses of German cavalry were reported in the neigh- bourhood of Lille, preceding hostile forces mov- ing through the district to the north of the Tourcoing-Armentiere line. This statement evidently refers to the same movement of German troops which is reported by a " Times " correspondent at Ostend, who estimates the number of this German force mov- ing through Belgium at 20,000 men. On the line of the flank battle that runs from Lassigny north to the neighbourhood of Arras minor successes and reverses alternated. on both sides. The general effect of the news was to show that the enemy's efforts to find a weak spot in this part of the field had completely failed. NEARING THE NORTH SEA. Reports from the French headquarters, pub- lished yesterday, were encouraging. On the Allied left flank, the t)attTp was developing favourably, particularly in the Arras reg,ion. Ground recently yielded in face of the violent German counter-attacks had been regained. Beyond .the main positions, which stretch con- tinually northward, the covering cavalry were approaching the North Sea coast. A LULL ON THE AISNE. While this great battle on the wing has been spreading towards the sea coast in Belgium, the forces on the line of the Aisne and on towards Alsace have been beating time, but there have been signs that the German strength was waning. "To the north of Soissons," the French reported, "we have made a slight ad- vance with the co-operation of the British Anny.”-

===== 0002601_19141010_art0181.txt | included=true | included =====
RUSSIAN VICTORY

CLEARING POLAND OF GERMANS.

The Russians have flung the German invaders of Russian Poland back into East Prussia. Not only have the Germans been thrown back from the Niemen in the direction of Suwalki and Augustowo, but the Russians have again in- vaded the Mazurian Lake region of Prussia by way of Grajewo. An attempted German offen- sive near 'Wawa was repulsed by the Russians.

===== 0002601_19141010_art0182.txt | included=true | included =====
GERNIANS AND AUSTRIANS JOIN FORCES.

Four Million Face Each Other. This clearing of the ground on the Russian right was a preliminary to the completion of the mustering of the Russian Armies for a combined advance against the power of Germany and Austria-Hungary. In the north their right wing, under General Rennenkampf; operating on the River Niemen, had turned the advance of the German troops from the East Prussian frontier into a retreat that threatened to develop into rout. The main Russian Army is posted on the Middle Vistula. Its right is in touch with Eennenkampf on the Niemen. Its left was covered by the armies of Generals Ruszky ad Brusseliloff advancing through Galicia. The Germans, evidently preparing to contest the advance of these Russian Armies along their whole front, arrested the retreat of the Austrian Armies and organised a composite host of Ger- man and Austrian troops, with which they pre- pared a battle that would rival the great engage- ments in France.

The Times military correspondent estimates the strength of the Germans and Austrians in this theatre at about 83 Army Corps. Russia has about 100 Army Corps, and vast reserves are hurrying up to their assistance. Not less than four million men will engage in the impending battle.

===== 0002601_19141010_art0183.txt | included=true | included =====
RUSSIANS CAN BEAT GERMANS.

Later news from Poland shows that General Rennenkampf inflicted very serious losses upon the Germans on the East Prussian frontier. About half a million men were engaged on each side, and, as the "Times" Petrograd correspon- dent reports, the importance of the victory is great, showing, as it does, that the Russians are I able to meet and defeat German troops as they have Austrian. The Germans under Hinden- burg were flushed with success. They had anni- hilated the too precipitate Russian advance in East Prussia, had raised the investment of Konigsberg, and had driven the invader not only over the East Prussian frontier, but be- yond the River Niemen. OFFICIAL REPORT.

The Great General Staff, Petrograd, issued the following communique : The battle of Augustowo ended in a victory

or the Russian arms. The German defeat is

complete. The enemy is now in disorderly re- treat, and is fleeing towards the frontier of Eastern Prussia. The valiant Russian troops are pressing vigorously on the heels of the Ger- mans, who in their frantic march are abandoning convoys, guns, and ammunition, and have not even time to collect their wounded.

Not content with vigorous counter-attacks, our troops took the offensive and pursued the retreating enemy. The Germans, despite the vigour of their resistance, could not arrest our advance. Our courageous troops carried the enemy's positions one after the other. Stag- gered by this irresistible action, the Germans broke and are now in retreat. They are leaving our territory in a hurry. At certain points the retreat is in the nature of a flight. We are vigorously pursuing the defeated army%. In the environs of Suwalki (north of Angus- towo), where the enemy concentrated very im- portant forces and where he had fortified the frontier, he offered a most desperate resistance. Oar troops made frequent bayonet assaults to I dislodge the Germans from the trenches. Pris- oners state that the German losses at Suwalki were so great that only twenty men remained out of detachments numbering a hundred. The whole line of the German retreat was encumbered by the bodies of their slain. This German style of invasion from Prussia has failed completely in Russia, and the enemy is now definitely leaving the limits and the pro- vinces of Suwalki and Lomza. The Russian General Staff at Pefrograd, in a summary of the position of the Russian armies, shows that the German Army defeated on the Niemen has retreated with much stub- born resistance to the East Prussian frontier. AUSTRIA'S TROUBLES.

Austria's troubles multiply. The Russians, who have almost (lestroyed the Dual Mon- archy's Armies in Galicia, are now over the Carpathians in sufficient force to fight consid- erable actions and take prisoners and guns. Their activity in Hungary may develop into an invasion formidable enough .to threaten Budapest and perhaps Vienna.

===== 0002601_19141010_art0184.txt | included=true | included =====
SOUTH AFRICA. ENTHUSIASM FOR :WAR POLICY.

South Africa's enthusiasm, evoked by the Government's decision to take a worthy part in the war and the announcement that General Botha would lead the Union forces in the field, increases. Meetings in support of the Govern- ment have unanimously approved the policy adopted by the Cabinet, while the pro-neutral- ity party has found difficulty in obtaining a public hearing. It has, however, seemed neces- sary for Mr. Burton, the Minister of Railways, to warn certain persons that toleration cannot• be extended to shelter German intrigue. On the Orange River frontier things have not gone entirely well. The losses in prisoners of the South African Mounted Rifles during the recently reported border reverse are now known to have numbered nearly 200. The Secretary of State for the Colonies pub- lished through the Press Bureau an account of a number of engagements which have taken place on the frontier of the British East African Protectorate. The enemy have, it appears, attempted several advances from German East Africa into the neighbouring British colonies. They have not met with much success. Superior forces when vigorously assailed by British detachments have fallen back with heavy losses, and the Uganda railway, the destruction of which was the enemy's main object, remains uninjured. Punjabi infantry, sent from India to rein- force the King's 'African Rifles, and the volun- teer units raised by the European settlers, have taken a gallant part in the fighting.

===== 0002601_19141010_art0185.txt | included=true | included =====
A GERMAN SUCCESS.

An account of the German success on the Namaqualand- frontier, when two squadrons of South African Mounted Rifles and a Transvaal Artillery Section were destroyed, has conie from Capetown. The union Force was sent forward to seize a watering station on the line of advance. There, while in a position diffi- cult to defend, the British and Dutch were attacked by Germans ten times more numerous than themselves, who had laid a trap for them. They offered a gallant resistance for hours and inflicted heavy losses on the enemy, but in the end were compelled to surrender.

===== 0002601_19141010_art0186.txt | included=true | included =====

SINKS A GERMAN DESTROYER.
